15 Trajectory Curation

Lecture slides for this chapter

Purpose

Agent stack focus for this chapter.

Why is an accepted agent trajectory not automatically a good training example?

Every agent run leaves a durable record of what it saw, proposed, and achieved, and a team preparing the next policy is tempted to train on that record directly. A record, however, answers a different question than a training example does. An accepted trial can contain a malformed call that the runtime refused, a failure can stem from a broken tool schema that no weight update repairs, and two attempts at the same task split across training and evaluation turn a test score into a measure of recall. Imitating such records teaches the model its own mistakes and workarounds, and filtering them by outcome alone narrows behavior toward whatever the filter happens to reward. Curation therefore decides, before any gradient is computed, whether a failure belongs in the weights at all, which records may serve which learner, which tokens are targets and which are context, and what evidence and permissions travel with every record derived from the original run.

Learning Objectives
  • Justify a learning use for a residual failure and distinguish imitation material, preference comparisons, and rollout fixtures
  • Design versioned collection fixtures and protected splits for a stated generalization claim
  • Construct admission predicates and compare verification costs without changing their criteria
  • Assign recovery and negative records to appropriate roles while distinguishing trajectory counts, stored tokens, and loss-bearing tokens
  • Assess how selection rules and sampling budgets change the diversity and yield of a self-improvement loop
  • Size bounded collection flow against downstream capacity, and price an emitted artifact by attempt cost and emission yield
  • Defend a versioned corpus manifest with transformation lineage, permitted uses, and intended consumers
Explore the concepts in this chapter

The volume’s concept map, opened on this chapter. Select a concept to read how it connects to ideas in other chapters. Open the full map ↗

15.1 From Trajectory Records to a Training Corpus

The evaluation harness of Agent Evaluation and the operational record of Operating Agents produced more than a release decision. While they judged the repair agent (Four lighthouse workloads) over repeated attempts on the connection-pool repair task, they also accumulated a durable trajectory log. The log captures every trial in granular detail: the environment observations delivered to the model, the code edits it proposed, the invalid tool calls rejected by the runtime, the subsequent recovery steps, and the final verdict scored against the task acceptance criteria. Some runs passed cleanly, others failed outright, and several remain stalled in pending states because regression suites or accounting checks timed out. To an engineering team preparing the next iteration of the policy, these accumulated interaction logs look like the natural, abundant raw material for fine-tuning.

Curation changes what the policy learns from its records and leaves every exposure where the runtime closes it.

The machinery built so far, however, does not specify how to translate raw logs into training data. The acceptance contract defined in The Task Acceptance Contract evaluates the final outcome of an entire trial rather than assigning credit to individual tokens or tool calls within it. Consequently, an accepted verdict alone cannot distinguish between an optimal tool call and an accidental stumble that the agent managed to survive. Imitating raw logs reproduces whatever they contain. If an agent wastes ten calls exploring invalid syntax before hitting the correct command, blind imitation trains the next model to repeat that exploratory churn. Furthermore, rigorous evaluation requires held-out task distributions that the training process never touches. If an engineer partitions data naively at the trace level, different attempts on the same underlying task fixture land on both sides of the train-test split, silently destroying evaluation integrity. Finally, because the agent generated these records under its own behavioral distribution, any filtering rule directly shapes what the subsequent policy will produce, inducing feedback dynamics across training generations.

Bridging the gap between raw execution logs and active model training requires a dedicated systems stage. Trajectory curation selects, labels, and transforms recorded interactions for a declared learning use while preserving the evidence and constraints that justify that use. Where evaluation establishes whether a trial satisfied its specification under fixed conditions, curation resolves a different operational question: which behaviors and tasks the learning process should absorb, which steps must be masked or rewritten, and how to preserve statistical isolation across splits. The end product of this pipeline is a versioned, reproducible corpus tailored to specific training consumers, accompanied by strict provenance records rather than an ad hoc collection of success flags. Transforming these admitted records into tokenized inputs and loss objectives becomes the downstream responsibility of Trajectory Fine-Tuning.

This chapter follows the repair agent from diagnosis to a published corpus. A failure caused by a truncated observation is the case whose remedy lies outside the weights, and a recovery from a refused edit becomes the record carried all the way into a corpus. The stages run in the order the risks arise. Before anything is collected, the failure must be shown to belong in the weights (section 15.2), and the evidence that will later judge the trained model must be fixed and protected (section 15.3, section 15.4). Collected records then pass admission checks specific to the learner that will consume them (section 15.5), recovery records are divided into context and targets (section 15.6), and the feedback created when a policy generates its own data is bounded (section 15.7). The remaining sections keep the collector within its capacity (section 15.8), bind every record to its lineage (section 15.9), and carry one record through the whole chain (section 15.10). The first decision is triage, because a failure that does not belong to the model should never reach a corpus.

15.2 Capability Gap Diagnosis

In Agent Evaluation, the repair agent’s trial on pool_repair_01 submitted workspace ws_r19 with its targeted reproducer passing, while regression and resource-accounting checks remained pending. The run was therefore neither an accepted repair nor a valid demonstration. A separate trial of the same task failed outright after the observation interface silently truncated a search result. The postmortem’s controlled ablation traced most of that failure to the interface: once pagination was added, every continuation from the saved prefix reached an accepted repair, even though the ablation did not prove the model blameless. A team that had collected those truncated-observation runs for training would have taught the model to work around an interface defect that a single adapter change removed. An execution failure belongs in the trajectory curation pipeline only after diagnosis confirms that model weights are the right target for repair. Modifying weights is justified only when confronting a genuine capability gap: an operational failure that persists even when the model receives complete context, interacts through a well-formed interface, and runs in a healthy runtime with adequate resources. Every other failure stems from an external system defect that calls for a cheaper, far more reversible engineering fix.

↩︎ The controlled ablation that traced the truncated-observation failure to the interface is established in Controlled diagnostic experiments.

15.2.1 Diagnosis and the learning objective

Separating a capability gap from an external system defect starts with decomposing the failing trace, because the final failure verdict on pool_repair_01 alone revealed nothing about which subsystem broke. The postmortem analysis in Incident Postmortems separated three distinct events within that truncated-observation incident: an interface defect (the search excerpt arrived without truncation metadata), a forbidden action proposal (the next response proposed replacing a file outside the repair scope), and a successful runtime refusal (the admission gate rejected that proposal before dispatch). Such findings often coexist within a single execution trace. Adding missing truncation metadata repairs the interface contract. Retaining the admission guard enforces system boundaries against out-of-scope dispatches. Training the policy to seek additional evidence addresses model behavior. Each diagnostic finding demands its own engineering remedy.

Table 1 connects diagnostic findings to candidate system interventions and their corresponding learning objectives. Because multiple failure modes frequently overlap in production traces, engineers must evaluate each candidate intervention under realistic operating conditions. The sequence in which teams investigate and apply these remedies follows directly from their computational cost and operational reversibility.

↩︎ Failure attribution, and the limit of what each finding shows, is established in Fault attribution.

15.2.2 The intervention ladder

Fine-tuning a model to compensate for an omitted context fact or an ambiguous tool schema is premature fine-tuning, an expensive waste of compute and curation effort on a defect that a prompt or schema fix cures in minutes. To prevent that misallocation, the intervention ladder orders failure remedies by ascending engineering cost and descending reversibility. Its rungs are context retrieval and assembly, tool definitions and schemas, runtime execution controls, supervised fine-tuning (updating weights to imitate selected demonstrations), and reinforcement learning (updating weights to raise a verified reward on fresh rollouts). Engineers test the inexpensive, reversible rungs first by default, advancing to a weight update only when diagnostic evidence establishes a genuine capability gap and confirms that training improves the behavior rather than masking an interface or runtime defect.

This ordering reflects how cost, reversibility, and enforcement guarantees change across the stack. Engineering and compute costs rise sharply at each rung. Editing a prompt assembly pipeline, schema definition, or runtime timeout modifies version-controlled configuration that an engineer can test against integration fixtures in minutes. Retraining weights, by contrast, requires curating a trajectory corpus, running accelerator jobs, and re-evaluating every capability the model serves rather than only the failing task.

Those cost differences are compounded by reversibility and blast radius. A defect repaired at a lower rung reverts cleanly as a configuration change whose impact is limited to the callers of the changed tool or prompt. Updating model weights, by contrast, alters behavior across the entire input space, and rolling back a checkpoint discards every concurrent improvement alongside the targeted fix. Reinforcement learning sits at the top of the ladder for the same reason: beyond the dataset and validation demands of supervised fine-tuning, it requires distributed rollout fleets, isolated reward verification harnesses, and optimization controls that keep the policy from exploiting loopholes in the reward check.

Even when a weight update succeeds, statistical weights cannot replace runtime enforcement. A fine-tuned policy may propose out-of-scope tool calls far less often, but lower observed failure rates do not guarantee compliance on every permitted input. Invariant closure (principle 1) requires that execution runtimes never assume policy compliance; resource budgets, authorization checks, and process sandboxes remain hard boundaries regardless of how thoroughly the model has been trained.

The end-to-end argument (Saltzer et al. 1984) yields the same conclusion for tool interfaces: a function belongs where it can be implemented completely and correctly. A typed action contract (principle 7) provides enforceable guarantees across any model version placed behind it. By contrast, fine-tuning a model to guess fields omitted by an API schema teaches brittle habits that break as soon as the interface changes. An integration test on an observation adapter can verify directly that truncated outputs return explicit pagination markers, resolving the ambiguity at the interface layer without asking downstream models to guess what was cut.

Empirical evidence, rather than administrative habit, decides when a failure climbs the ladder. Engineers test each lower rung with a counterfactual probe, a controlled re-execution of the recorded failure trace with that rung’s suspected defect removed. A context probe begins by injecting the missing fact directly into the prompt. An interface probe resumes matched saved prefixes with corrected tool descriptions or schemas and samples fresh continuations under the controlled conditions of Statistical Evaluation Rigor (since replaying a fixed proposal would test only the validation or adapter boundary, not whether the model chooses a different action). If both checks leave the failure intact, a runtime probe runs the identical tool payload inside an isolated sandbox with relaxed resource limits. Whenever a failure disappears under one of these probes, the result supports a repair at that rung under the probed conditions without ruling out other contributing causes. Probing in ladder order routes each resolved defect to an immediate, reversible repair, so only the failures that survive all three probes reach the weights (figure 1). Even then, checkpoints trained on this residual must pass the evaluation protocol of Statistical Evaluation Rigor rather than ship merely because model weights were the last hypothesis left.

The intervention ladder is a diagnostic discipline rather than a rigid queue. When counterfactual probes immediately expose a capability gap in an agent that fails despite complete context, a clear schema, and a healthy runtime, engineers can skip lower-rung changes and proceed directly to targeted training, provided the empirical evidence of the gap is recorded first. Moreover, production environments routinely return partial outputs, transient network timeouts, and service-unavailable errors; training an agent to handle those degraded conditions is a valid behavioral objective, not an excuse to leave broken interfaces unfixed. Notebook 15.1 prices the compute and reliability trade-offs across this hierarchy for a representative failure workload.

Napkin Math 15.1: Pricing the ladder for one failure mix
Problem: A hypothetical maintenance agent executes 50,000 trajectories per month, with 6 percent failing (3,000 total failures). Counterfactual probes attribute 70 percent of the failures to a tool schema that omits a required field and 20 percent to sandbox startup timeouts; only the remaining failures survive every probe. One engineering team repairs the tool schema and scales the sandbox pool before considering training. A second team bypasses system repairs and fine-tunes the base model on all failing trajectories using corrected demonstration targets, training on 4,096-token sequences across 3 epochs. What failure modes does each path eliminate, and what is the direct compute cost of the training run?

Variables: The underlying model contains 70.6 billion parameters. Standard transformer training requires approximately 6 floating-point operations (FLOPs) per parameter per token (\(6\Parameters \DatasetSize\)). A dedicated cluster node of 8 accelerators provides a dense 16-bit compute peak of 989 TFLOP/s per device while sustaining 30 percent model FLOPs utilization (MFU). Accelerator rental costs $4 per hour. Both paths assume that diagnostic attribution is correct and exclude data curation, annotation, and software engineering labor.

Math: System-level repairs eliminate the 2,700 failures attributed to interface and runtime defects (90 percent of all failures), deploying and reverting cleanly as versioned configuration updates. In contrast, fine-tuning indiscriminately across all 3,000 failures trains on 3,000 \(\times\) 4,096 \(\times\) 3 = 36,864,000 tokens. Evaluating \(6 \times \text{parameters} \times \text{tokens}\) yields 1.56 × 10¹⁹ FLOPs of total training compute. At sustained cluster throughput, this workload requires 1.8 h of wall-clock time (14.6 accelerator-hours), resulting in an accelerator invoice of $58. Fine-tuning exclusively on the 300 residual failures that survived diagnostic probing costs only $6.

Result: The direct accelerator invoice of $58 is modest, so raw compute expenditure does not govern intervention ordering. The decisive factor is the architectural debt and reliability profile left in production. Under premature fine-tuning, the tool schema still omits the required field and the sandbox pool still times out under load; consequently, those operational failures return whenever the model guesses incorrectly or cluster load spikes. Furthermore, teaching the model to work around missing fields creates fragile behavioral compensations that collapse as soon as the interface changes. Its training corpus also squanders curation and review effort on incidents whose proper remedy was never behavioral. Under the systems repair path, engineers resolve 90 percent of all failures permanently for every model checkpoint, leaving a residual of 300 failures whose curated corpus is focused entirely on genuine policy gaps.

Systems insight: The primary risk of premature fine-tuning is rarely compute expenditure; it is architectural misattribution. While an invoice of $58 appears manageable, training cannot fix the 90 percent of failures rooted below the weights. Only a systems repair resolves those defects permanently across all current and future model generations. The governing engineering principle is to attribute failures with counterfactual probes first, remediate what lies below the weights in software, and train exclusively on the residual capability gap that survives.

Once diagnostic probing isolates a genuine residual failure, engineers must specify the exact behavior to train before gathering examples. For the repair agent, the paginated interface now supplies completeness metadata, but a policy can still ignore it. Runs on the repaired interface that receive that metadata and still draw a conclusion from a partial result form the residual that a learning objective can target. An effective objective does not aim vaguely to “fix failed trajectories.” Instead, it trains the policy to inspect the completeness metadata of a search result, issue a permitted follow-up query when pagination indicates more records exist, and state remaining data boundaries when limits are reached. Evaluating the fine-tuned model requires checking both the final task outcome and the efficiency of the path, including redundant reads, context token use, and unsupported intermediate claims. A policy can easily learn a circuitous investigation habit that raises task acceptance at the cost of high latency and token spend, or an overly aggressive shortcut that skips required checks; neither flaw shows up in cross-entropy training loss alone.

15.2.3 Learning artifacts

Targeting that residual behavior does not mean keeping only clean, end-to-end successes, because a pipeline that discards every flawed run throws away most of its training signal. A trace that stumbled on a malformed tool call before recovering still demonstrates how to repair the mistake, and a run that failed outright on a subtle race condition still captures a valuable test environment. Depending on what evidence a trace preserves, a single recorded interaction can feed three distinct learning artifacts, each governed by its own admission criteria.

Imitation material consists of complete demonstrations or extracted trajectory segments whose decision steps represent exemplary behavior under the supplied context. When curating an error-recovery sequence, the ingestion pipeline can retain an initial malformed tool proposal and its runtime rejection within the conversational history, while selecting only the subsequent corrected action as the supervised target. Faithfully preserving an error in conversational context provides realism, but supervising a model to imitate an unnecessary retry loop degrades runtime efficiency. Curation explicitly designates the target tokens to be reinforced.

A preference comparison pairs alternative trajectories generated from a shared initial context, attaching an explicit, justified preference ordering. Two runs of a task form a valid comparison pair only when their initial state, environment configuration, and accessible action spaces are identical. Furthermore, a failing or degraded run belongs in the rejected slot only when an evaluator records explicit diagnostic criteria explaining why its trajectory was inferior. These supporting assessments, tie-breaking criteria, and conditioning contexts allow offline preference optimization methods to construct statistically sound loss objectives.

↪ The trajectory preference loss, which scores the preferred and dispreferred records against a frozen reference policy, is developed in The trajectory preference loss.

A rollout fixture bundles a reproducible task environment, versioned configuration fixtures, and an automated grading specification to support online exploration during reward-based reinforcement learning. An unsuccessful execution attempt often reveals a valuable environmental corner case even when none of its recorded actions warrant imitation. In this setting, the trace does not provide demonstration data; rather, it seeds a versioned simulation fixture that recreates the problematic state so the policy can explore alternative trajectories and optimize against the reward specification.

↪ Optimizing a policy against verifiable checks on the rollouts it executes in such an environment is developed in Foundations of Verifiable Rewards.

These three downstream roles explain why a curation pipeline preserves execution failures rather than discarding them. A single flawed trajectory can support diagnostic root-cause analysis, provide multi-turn context for an error-recovery demonstration, serve as a negative example within a preference comparison, or seed a simulation fixture for online reinforcement learning. The intended downstream artifact dictates the admission evidence required to validate the record. Symmetrically, successful trajectories that produce no state mutations (an agent correctly refusing an out-of-scope request or answering a read-only query) constitute valid imitation demonstrations whenever the task specification requires restraint. Producing a nonempty workspace modification is never an inherent admission requirement.

Every proposed trajectory collection campaign must formally document four elements: the specific behavioral capability to be trained, the target artifact format, the empirical evidence required for admission, and the held-out evaluation claim that will assess the trained model. When trajectory evaluations rely on automated model judges or human annotators, those evaluations must operate under explicit rubrics with evaluator coverage and scoring variance fully exposed.

Curation produces defensible artifacts for declared uses, while held-out evaluation decides whether the resulting model update actually improves the system. That separation is the decision boundary of verified trajectory post-training (principle 13), which holds that a recorded outcome does not by itself determine how a trajectory may be used for learning. Two obligations follow before any record is collected. Fixtures must translate the desired behavior into collectible tasks, and the evaluation split must be reserved first, so that data selection never consumes the evidence later used to judge it.

15.3 Task Fixture Design

When an agent collection worker attempts a complex task such as connection-pool repair repeatedly, it can generate a distinct candidate solution on every run. If an attempt inherits residual state from an earlier repair attempt, however, it no longer demonstrates solving the problem from the declared initial state. Conversely, if a collection pipeline records only the final passing patch, it discards the sequence of tool invocations, intermediate terminal outputs, and error-recovery decisions that explain how that patch was derived. A collection fixture prevents both failures by preserving reproducible task conditions while recording the comprehensive execution evidence demanded by downstream learning algorithms.

The task fixture of Task fixtures already fixes the execution baseline (initial state, dependencies, permitted interfaces, variation policy, evidence boundary, and reset). Trajectory curation layers a collection contract on top of that fixture. The collection contract specifies why the task belongs in the corpus, which interaction records the ingestion pipeline may retain, and what intermediate evidence downstream admission filters will require.

15.3.1 The collection manifest

If a new collection run on pool_repair_01 reuses the trial identity of ws_r19, overwrites the unresolved record of test_r18, or lets engineers adjust admission rules after inspecting the sampled outputs, the pipeline corrupts its own audit trail. Separating the versioned identities of the task specification (pool_repair_01), execution fixture (pool_fixture_v1), and evaluation criteria (repair_accept_v1) prevents that collision. Each new collection attempt receives its own run identity and produces an independent candidate artifact, while the historical evaluation candidate ws_r19 stays permanently bound to its original trial, and a newly passing test run never alters an earlier unresolved record such as test_r18.

The collection manifest references the underlying evaluation fixture and records the operational decisions summarized in table 2. Defining these fields before sampling begins ensures that data engineers fix the intended training role and capture requirements in advance, rather than biasing the sample or fitting admission rules retroactively to observed outputs.

What the manifest must capture depends on the consumer that will train on the artifact. A demonstration selected for supervised error recovery must preserve the diagnostic context, error messages, and intermediate inspection steps that explain why a corrective action was taken. An offline preference comparison needs matched decision conditions and environment state across paired rollouts so that the comparison isolates policy differences. A fixture built for online reinforcement learning rollouts must instead expose live, resettable environment instances and versioned reward harnesses rather than static text logs. Because the target loss function decides how admitted trajectory data is tokenized and masked, a trace that lacks certain tool outputs may fail admission for imitation yet remain useful for failure diagnosis. Curation pipelines therefore tailor their logging schemas to the declared learning objective rather than relying on a single untyped event log, and verify that the grading harness itself is trustworthy before collection begins.

15.3.2 Adequate and inadequate controls

An automated grader that accepts an invalid solution pollutes a training corpus with false-positive demonstrations, while a grader that rejects valid work starves the collection pipeline. Before launching data collection at fleet scale, engineers calibrate grading harnesses against independently verified adequate and inadequate controls, candidate implementations whose expected verdicts are known in advance.

↩︎ Measuring a grader’s false-acceptance rate against independently assessed calibration cases is established in Grader error rates.

For the connection-pool repair task, an inadequate control is a candidate that deliberately keeps the target defect, such as leaving worker threads blocked after a connection returns to the pool (the missed-wakeup bug). This buggy candidate must reliably trigger a failure in the reproducer suite. Conversely, an adequate control is an independently reviewed reference patch that resolves the issue cleanly; it must consistently pass the test harness. If the reproducer accepts the buggy control or rejects the reference repair, the failure points to a flaw in the test harness, flaky environment state, or broken process exit bindings that must be fixed before collection proceeds. Each control isolates and tests a specific grading criterion. A run that produces the reference repair must satisfy the full suite of regression, execution, resource, and residual-duty checks defined in repair_accept_v1. Separate controls check that the agent does not alter protected configuration files or emit premature completion signals.

The harness that grades collection runs executes outside the agent’s authority boundary, satisfying invariant closure (principle 1). It keeps the grader isolation of Grader isolation, so candidate code cannot rewrite tests, forge exit codes, or read hidden assets, and section 15.5.2 develops what admission adds to that boundary. Whenever engineers modify a fixture, update a grading rubric, or shift the target task distribution, they validate the changes against fresh control suites rather than inheriting prior trust.

Controls matter equally for tasks that change no persistent state. Consider a read-only evaluation fixture, report_comparison_v1, containing two immutable experiment reports. Report A documents that an optimization method was evaluated on Linux; Report B documents that an alternative method was evaluated on Windows. Neither report contains comparative throughput or latency measurements. The agent’s prompt asks for the documented platform coverage and asks whether the reports show which method achieves higher throughput. The task’s grading criteria demand accurate, source-specific attribution, an explicit statement that the throughput comparison is unsupported by the evidence, and strict compliance with read-only access limits.

Here, an adequate control is an independently validated response that maps each platform to its source and declines to declare a throughput winner. Inadequate controls include responses that swap the platform attributions, invent throughput claims, or return an empty answer that omits the documented facts. Because the filesystem stays bit-for-bit identical across all runs, diff-based checks cannot evaluate success; the grading harness must instead run structured field assertions and rubric checks to verify factual attribution and confirm that the agent stated the limits of the evidence. Automated curation can therefore harvest examples of grounded abstention without granting write permissions or requiring workspace patches, across whatever trajectory sources feed the collector.

15.3.3 Trajectory sources

Relying on a single source of trajectories leaves systematic blind spots in a training corpus: production logs overrepresent routine queries and abandoned user sessions, human demonstrations are too slow to cover wide fault matrices, and synthetic rollouts repeat whatever strategy the current policy already favors. Trajectory harvesting therefore draws from all three origins (human demonstrations, production telemetry, and synthetic model rollouts), balancing what each contributes against the collection risk it carries (table 3).

These three sources complement one another across the lifecycle of a training pipeline. A production failure reveals a real-world edge case that warrants reproduction in a benchmark. A human demonstration provides an expert recovery strategy to seed the model. Synthetic rollouts explore fault matrices through automated variation. When generating those synthetic rollouts, the environment must compute state transitions dynamically. If an agent emits an action that diverges from a historical trace, the platform cannot replay the next observation from a recorded log; doing so breaks environment causality and feeds contradictory context into the trajectory. Synthetic collection requires a live, interactive environment that generates valid observations for whatever action sequence the policy proposes.

The choice of data source shapes the distribution of harvested behaviors. A collection policy that filters strictly for short, successful runs discards trajectories that show backtracking and fault recovery under partial observability, whereas relying uncritically on production traces overrepresents abandoned sessions and unresolved timeouts. Recording the collection policy, sampling parameters, and task-family metadata in the manifest lets downstream curation stages distinguish genuinely rare engineering events from artificial gaps created by collection filters, and sets up the question of how broadly the task fixtures themselves span the domain.

15.3.4 Task population coverage

A repair corpus collected only on shallow, single-file bugs with clear stack traces produces a policy that stalls as soon as production code hides a race condition behind deep dependencies or ambiguous test output. To match operational conditions, task distributions must vary systematically across the dimensions that make tasks hard in practice. In software repair, fixtures should vary across dependency tree depth, the clarity of error feedback before an edit, and the concurrency conditions encountered during testing. In document synthesis, fixtures should deliberately include contradictory sources, missing numerical metrics, and schema drift across versions.

The H·S·A exposures (The H·S·A Exposure Space) characterize each variant: the horizon \(\TurnHorizon\) in turns required to solve the task, the state level (\(\StateExposureLevel_0\) to \(\StateExposureLevel_3\)) the task retains or mutates across steps, and the authority level (\(\AuthorityExposureLevel_0\) to \(\AuthorityExposureLevel_3\)) its tool set grants. Varying a task along these exposures provides a concrete basis for task variation that avoids arbitrary context-length thresholds or the false assumption that all meaningful tasks must mutate a filesystem.

System privileges and tool access grants remain explicit and enforced across all task variants. As a model improves, it may learn to query fewer tools, avoid redundant exploratory commands, or complete tasks in fewer turns. That progress changes the agent’s realized exposure to the environment, but the underlying permissions and runtime sandboxing boundaries stay fixed. Similarly, the environment reset contract governs every reuse of a fixture. If a failed run leaves background processes running, uncommitted file locks, or open network sockets, teardown must clear this residual state before the next collection worker starts, whether the previous trajectory was admitted into the corpus or discarded.

↩︎ The reset contract, which catalogs every piece of state that teardown must clear before an environment serves a new lease, is established in The reset contract.

A well-designed collection fixture unifies the task specification and grading criteria with an explicit learning objective, a capture contract, and a structured distribution of variants. Before collection workers run across this task matrix, however, the held-out evaluation tasks must be partitioned and quarantined, so that no collected record can reach the evidence that will later judge the trained model. Reserving those tasks is the focus of section 15.4.

15.4 Split Design

Consider an automated collection pipeline that harvests multiple repair trajectories for the same connection-pool defect. If an engineer naively partitions the resulting execution traces at random, one successful repair trajectory lands in the training set while another attempt at the exact same defect lands in the final test set. Varying the random seed, prompt wording, or tool sequence creates superficial operational diversity, but it does not make the underlying engineering problem new. When the fine-tuned policy subsequently encounters this task during evaluation, it does not demonstrate generalization; it merely recalls an environment, bug mechanism, and solution it has already observed during training. This failure mode is split leakage: information about an evaluation target reaching the training pipeline through a related or derived record (Kaufman et al. 2012).

1 Duplication in Code Corpora: Code datasets are especially prone to leakage because repository forks, vendored libraries, and copy-pasted snippets reproduce the same defect across nominally distinct codebases. Evaluating code models on corpora that contain near-duplicates inflates reported accuracy by as much as a factor of two compared with deduplicated corpora (Allamanis 2019). An agent trajectory corpus inherits every duplicate in the underlying codebases and adds a second source of duplication: repeated execution rollouts on the same benchmark fixture. Grouping trajectories by their underlying task is therefore required for valid evaluation rather than an optional refinement.

Allamanis, Miltiadis. 2019. “The Adverse Effects of Code Duplication in Machine Learning Models of Code.” Proceedings of the 2019 ACM SIGPLAN International Symposium on New Ideas, New Paradigms, and Reflections on Programming and Software (Onward!), 143–53. https://doi.org/10.1145/3359591.3359735.

In agent trajectory curation, split leakage is the default outcome rather than a rare edge case.1 Suppose an agent executes 8 rollouts per task and an engineer partitions the individual traces at random using a 80 percent/20 percent ratio. A task remains cleanly isolated on one side of the split only if every one of its attempts independently falls into that same partition. For a training fraction \(\TrainingSplitFraction\) and \(\IndependentAttemptCount\) independent attempts per task, that non-leakage probability is \(\TrainingSplitFraction^\IndependentAttemptCount + (1-\TrainingSplitFraction)^\IndependentAttemptCount\). Consequently, 83.2 percent of all tasks straddle the train–test boundary, contaminating the evaluation protocol. Partitioning schemes must therefore follow structural task relationships rather than arbitrary trace run identifiers.

With eight attempts per task, a random 80/20 trace split leaves 83 percent of tasks on both sides.

While the fixture contract defines what an individual execution trial represents, split design governs what subsequent evaluations can validly claim about agent learning. To preserve that validity, engineers isolate and lock down protected evaluation tasks before data collection, model-assisted filtering, or hyperparameter tuning begins. If developers inspect final test failures and then harvest demonstrations targeting those specific failure modes, they turn the test suite into training feedback, and the benchmark ceases to be an independent evaluation even when no raw test trajectory enters the training manifest.

15.4.1 Evaluation claims and task groups

A split that holds out individual bug tickets within a single repository cannot test whether a fine-tuned agent transfers to an unseen codebase. A split that holds out entire repository families, by contrast, is unnecessarily restrictive when the agent will only ever serve one internal monorepo. Every evaluation split must therefore begin with an explicit claim about the capability under test. An in-distribution deployment check asks whether an adapted agent can resolve new maintenance tickets within the repositories and runtime environments it already serves. An out-of-distribution transfer check asks whether that agent succeeds across unseen repository families, software architectures, or tool environments. Familiarity with a shared build tool, compiler flags, or API schema reflects legitimate deployment conditions and is not leakage. Evaluating an agent on a defect whose solution appeared during fine-tuning, however, invalidates the measurement under either claim.

To enforce these boundaries systematically, the curation pipeline organizes tasks into groups. A task group collects all repeated attempts, paraphrased prompts, derived test fixtures, and solution-equivalent variations of a single underlying problem. If a benchmark contains an issue fixture pool_repair_01, any rollout generated from an alternate random seed, a renamed variable, or an identical upstream bug copied into a downstream fork belongs to that same task group. The split manifest records the structural reason for each grouping because simple heuristics fail in opposite directions. Grouping solely by repository name misses cross-repository forks and vendored copies. Grouping by shared dependencies merges unrelated software defects into giant clusters.

Formally, let the group map \(\TaskGroupMap(\EnvironmentFixture)\) send an environment fixture \(\EnvironmentFixture\) to its designated task group, and let the split map \(\SplitAssignmentMap(\TaskGroupMap)\) assign that group to a partition: training, development, or final test. Every harvested trajectory, synthesized record, or distilled learning example inherits the partition assignment of its originating fixture. Partition integrity requires that:

\[\TaskGroupMap(\EnvironmentFixture_i)=\TaskGroupMap(\EnvironmentFixture_j) \implies \SplitAssignmentMap(\TaskGroupMap(\EnvironmentFixture_i))=\SplitAssignmentMap(\TaskGroupMap(\EnvironmentFixture_j)).\]

This partitioning invariant guarantees that every known form of a problem stays on one side of the split. Because the guarantee is only as strong as the pipeline’s record of how tasks relate, production split manifests log the active grouping policy, git commit lineages, schema versions, and quarantined matches awaiting human triage. When allocating splits, randomization operates over task groups rather than individual rollouts, stratifying by task complexity or repository size where needed. Evaluation dashboards report group counts alongside raw trajectory counts, because dozens of rollouts on one fixture still test only one problem.

Table 4 maps common evaluation claims to their required isolation boundaries. Every valid protocol enforces task-group isolation at a minimum.

Engineering teams must also keep development and final-test partitions separate across iterations. Development tasks guide day-to-day tuning of prompts, trajectory filters, and fine-tuning mixtures, while final-test tasks provide a single measurement of the finished system under the declared protocol. Evaluating candidate checkpoints repeatedly against the final test suite and adjusting data mixtures from those scores leaks test signal into the training loop. Whenever a team inspects a final-test failure to diagnose a model flaw or build a training demonstration, it must reclassify that task group as development data and draw an unseen task group to replace it in the final test. Keeping that boundary intact requires controlling every path through which held-out evidence could reach upstream components.

15.4.2 Held-out evidence protection

If a synthetic problem generator reads a held-out test specification, it weaves the target logic into nominally new training fixtures. If an automated trajectory ranker scores candidates by their performance on the final test suite, it tunes the training corpus to that test set without copying a single token. A partition boundary is therefore an architectural firewall across information paths, not merely a directory tag on a storage volume. Reference patches, ground-truth task specifications, hidden unit tests, and final evaluation outcomes must stay isolated from every upstream component: trajectory harvesting prompts, vector retrieval indexes, shared cross-session agent memories, synthetic generators, and model-based selection filters. During a trial itself, the agent likewise receives only the feedback the test protocol permits, such as local development tests, while the hidden acceptance suite stays behind the grader isolation of Grader isolation. Protecting held-out evidence means auditing every one of these access paths across both upstream curation pipelines and the trial runtime.

Whenever new candidate fixtures enter the curation pipeline, ingestion checks compare them against reserved task groups using task identifiers, git commit histories, patch hashes, and abstract syntax trees. Any fixture with ambiguous lineage goes to a quarantine queue for manual review rather than receiving a fresh identifier. Redaction, variable renaming, and schema changes do not break provenance; derived records always inherit their parent fixture’s group assignment. If a later audit finds that training data leaked information about a final-test task, deleting the offending record from the data lake is not enough. Because the leaked signal is already in the weights, the team must retire the checkpoint, re-evaluate any affected claims, and retrain from a clean corpus snapshot.

Even when local split boundaries are airtight, pipelines remain exposed to contamination: the appearance of evaluation tasks or solutions inside the base model’s pretraining web crawl.2 Public benchmarks drawn from competitive programming sites or popular open-source repositories often sit inside pretraining corpora, which undermines zero-shot or few-shot generalization claims. Defensible evaluation therefore relies on private fixtures authored behind internal firewalls, commit timestamps audited against model training cutoffs, and documented data provenance, backed by similarity screening to catch unrecorded duplicates before splits are frozen.

2 Benchmark Contamination: When web-scale pretraining corpora grew to include public benchmarks, models were often trained directly on the test splits later used to grade them (Sainz et al. 2023). Because base-model providers rarely publish token-level manifests of their pretraining data, downstream teams must rely on n-gram overlap probes and memorization checks to detect leakage. For an agent trajectory pipeline, the only reliable defense is structural: maintain private internal fixtures whose code and solutions have never appeared on the public web, and report separately which benchmark results come from those private evaluations.

Sainz, Oscar, Jon Campos, Iker Garcı́a-Ferrero, Julen Etxaniz, Oier Lopez de Lacalle, and Eneko Agirre. 2023. “NLP Evaluation in Trouble: On the Need to Measure LLM Data Contamination for Each Benchmark.” Findings of the Association for Computational Linguistics: EMNLP 2023, 10776–87. https://doi.org/10.18653/v1/2023.findings-emnlp.722.

↩︎ Contamination is one form of the pre-trial leakage established in Evaluation leakage.

15.4.3 Similarity screening

Metadata group assignments catch only the relationships the pipeline already knows; a vendored library or a bug copied across unrelated repositories without a shared git commit slips right past them. Similarity screening searches for those unrecorded relatives so they can join their task group before splits are assigned, and the same screen limits redundancy inside the training corpus. In language-model pretraining, removing near-duplicate documents cuts verbatim memorization and improves generalization (Lee et al. 2022). In agent trajectory curation, however, similarity depends on how a record is represented. Purely textual overlap misleads in both directions. Two repairs can fix the same race condition with almost no shared lines in their patch diffs. Two traces that run the same shell commands and test invocations can diverge completely in how they read the terminal output and recover from errors.

Lee, Katherine, Daphne Ippolito, Andrew Nystrom, Chiyuan Zhang, Douglas Eck, Chris Callison-Burch, and Nicholas Carlini. 2022. “Deduplicating Training Data Makes Language Models Better.” Proceedings of the 60th Annual Meeting of the Association for Computational Linguistics (Volume 1: Long Papers), 8424–45. https://doi.org/10.18653/v1/2022.acl-long.577.

3 MinHash: Minwise independent permutations were introduced to spot near-duplicate web pages at search-engine scale, where comparing every pair of documents directly is far too slow (Broder 1997). Trajectory screening shares the same economics: signatures are cheap to compute and store, and the real cost lies in reviewing the candidate pairs they surface.

Broder, A. Z. 1997. “On the Resemblance and Containment of Documents.” Proceedings. Compression and Complexity of SEQUENCES 1997 (Cat. No.97TB100171), 21–29. https://doi.org/10.1109/sequen.1997.666900.

To capture meaningful similarity in agent trajectories, feature extraction combines text shingles, parsed code structure, and tool-call sequences. A shingle is a local feature extracted from a document or trace, such as an n-gram of command tokens or a subtree from an abstract syntax tree (AST). While parsing code into an AST strips away formatting differences, aggressive canonicalization (discarding variable names, literal constants, or statement order) can erase the difference between a working patch and a broken one. Curation pipelines therefore version their normalization rules and treat high similarity scores as prompts for inspection rather than triggers for automatic deletion. To compare feature sets across hundreds of thousands of trajectories, pipelines use MinHash (Broder 1997),3 which compresses large feature sets into short signatures by taking the minimum hash value under each of several random permutations. Workloads where feature counts matter use weighted or bag-based variants instead.

For nonempty finite feature sets \(\ScreenedFeatureSet_1\) and \(\ScreenedFeatureSet_2\), the Jaccard similarity \(\JaccardSimilarity(\ScreenedFeatureSet_1,\ScreenedFeatureSet_2)\) and the theoretical MinHash collision probability satisfy:

\[\JaccardSimilarity(\ScreenedFeatureSet_1,\ScreenedFeatureSet_2)=\frac{|\ScreenedFeatureSet_1\cap \ScreenedFeatureSet_2|}{|\ScreenedFeatureSet_1\cup \ScreenedFeatureSet_2|}, \qquad \Pr\!\left(\min_{\ScreenedFeature\in \ScreenedFeatureSet_1}\MinHashPermutation(\ScreenedFeature)=\min_{\ScreenedFeature\in \ScreenedFeatureSet_2}\MinHashPermutation(\ScreenedFeature)\right)=\JaccardSimilarity(\ScreenedFeatureSet_1,\ScreenedFeatureSet_2).\]

Here, \(\MinHashPermutation\) is a uniformly random permutation of the feature vocabulary. Under \(\MinHashPermutation\), every element of the union \(\ScreenedFeatureSet_1\cup \ScreenedFeatureSet_2\) is equally likely to receive the smallest rank. When that smallest element lies in the intersection \(\ScreenedFeatureSet_1\cap \ScreenedFeatureSet_2\), it belongs to both sets and becomes the minimum of each, whereas an element belonging to only one set leaves the other set with a strictly larger minimum. Each permutation therefore acts as a binary trial that produces a match with probability \(|\ScreenedFeatureSet_1\cap \ScreenedFeatureSet_2|/|\ScreenedFeatureSet_1\cup \ScreenedFeatureSet_2|\). Across \(\MinHashPermutationCount\) independent random permutations, the fraction of matching signature components averages those independent binary indicators, forming an unbiased estimator of \(\JaccardSimilarity(\ScreenedFeatureSet_1,\ScreenedFeatureSet_2)\) with variance \(\JaccardSimilarity(1-\JaccardSimilarity)/\MinHashPermutationCount\) (Li and Li 2022). Production systems approximate true permutations with universal hash families and define explicit fallback rules for empty feature sets.

Li, Xiaoyun, and Ping Li. 2022. “C-MinHash: Improving Minwise Hashing with Circulant Permutation.” International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML) 162: 12857–87.

4 Locality-Sensitive Hashing: A hashing scheme in which the collision probability increases with item similarity, built for sublinear approximate nearest-neighbor search in high dimensions (Indyk and Motwani 1998). Banding MinHash signatures adapts LSH to Jaccard similarity. Engineers choose \(\SignatureBandCount\) and \(\LshRowsPerBand\) to match candidate volume to downstream review capacity, since large collision buckets can still make pair-review work grow rapidly with corpus size.

Indyk, Piotr, and Rajeev Motwani. 1998. “Approximate Nearest Neighbors: Towards Removing the Curse of Dimensionality.” Proceedings of the Thirtieth Annual ACM Symposium on Theory of Computing (STOC), 604–13. https://doi.org/10.1145/276698.276876.

Comparing every pair of MinHash signatures directly still scales quadratically with corpus size, so pipelines index signatures with Locality-Sensitive Hashing (LSH).4 An LSH index splits an \(\MinHashPermutationCount\)-component signature into \(\SignatureBandCount\) bands of \(\LshRowsPerBand\) rows each (\(\MinHashPermutationCount = \SignatureBandCount \LshRowsPerBand\)). Two trajectories become candidate near-duplicates if their signatures match across all \(\LshRowsPerBand\) rows in at least one band. With independent hash components, a pair with Jaccard similarity \(\JaccardSimilarity\) matches all rows of a given band with probability \(\JaccardSimilarity^{\LshRowsPerBand}\), and collides in at least one of the \(\SignatureBandCount\) bands with probability:

\[\LshCandidateProbability=1-(1-\JaccardSimilarity^{\LshRowsPerBand})^\SignatureBandCount.\]

This function forms an S-shaped selection curve whose steep region sits near \((1/\SignatureBandCount)^{1/\LshRowsPerBand}\). With the signature length \(\MinHashPermutationCount = \SignatureBandCount \LshRowsPerBand\) held fixed, the two parameters trade off directly. Moving components from rows into bands (fewer rows per band, more bands) shifts the curve toward lower similarities, which catches more potential duplicates at the cost of larger collision buckets and heavier downstream review. Adding bands without reducing rows shifts the curve the same way while lengthening every signature.

Banding transforms candidate retrieval into an S-shaped probability curve governed by the number of bands and rows.

With \(\MinHashPermutationCount =\) 128 independent ideal components, the variance \(\JaccardSimilarity(1-\JaccardSimilarity)/\MinHashPermutationCount\) peaks at \(\JaccardSimilarity = 0.5\), so the standard error is at most \(0.5/\sqrt{\MinHashPermutationCount}\), or 0.0442 on the Jaccard scale. Storing each component in 4 bytes requires 512 bytes per signature and 51.2 MB for 100,000 records. While this raw matrix excludes the feature inverted index, metadata storage, and candidate staging buffers, it shows that signature storage is small even for large corpora.

The index configuration of 32 bands with 4 rows retrieves a pair with similarity \(\JaccardSimilarity =\) 0.5 with candidate probability 87.3 percent, and candidate retrieval becomes virtually certain at \(\JaccardSimilarity =\) 0.8. Downstream review capacity, whether automated AST diff verification or human inspection, is the binding constraint. Because this configuration routes most moderately similar pairs to review, engineers tune the band geometry to their review budget rather than to an abstract similarity target.

Engineers must inspect candidate pairs in context before merging task groups or discarding trajectories from the corpus. Trajectory deduplication differs from document deduplication: a short, three-step execution trace is not automatically better than a longer trace that shows error recovery, and two agents that produce identical code patches may have taken distinct decision paths that are both worth keeping. Once every known and screened relative is locked into its group’s partition, the admission cascade can evaluate each candidate record on its own evidence.

15.5 Staged Verifier Cascades

Even after a trajectory record is locked into a valid split partition and captures a passing bug fix, it remains unusable for imitation learning if its data licensing review is incomplete or its execution trace contains unredacted credentials. Running a heavy, multi-minute containerized test suite before verifying those compliance gates wastes expensive verification slots on candidates the system must ultimately reject. Curation architectures therefore separate two decisions: they define the admission criteria required for each learning objective first, and then schedule the predicates in an order that minimizes wasted checking work.5

5 Classifier Cascade: An ordered sequence of tests that drops unpromising candidates early using cheap checks gained prominence in real-time object detection, where a cascade of boosted classifiers rejected background image patches after computing only a few features (Viola and Jones 2001). A curation cascade shares the same compute economics, but its error tolerance is much stricter: whereas a visual detector can trade a small rise in false positives for faster throughput, loosening an admission check in a curation cascade pollutes the training corpus and degrades the resulting policy.

Viola, Paul, and Michael J. Jones. 2001. “Rapid Object Detection Using a Boosted Cascade of Simple Features.” Proceedings of the 2001 IEEE Computer Society Conference on Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition. CVPR 2001 1: I-511-I-518. https://doi.org/10.1109/cvpr.2001.990517.
Definition 15.1: Verifier cascade

Verifier cascade, also called an admission cascade, is an ordered sequence of declared verification predicates that screens a candidate trajectory for inclusion in a specific learning dataset, terminating evaluation along that route at the first unsatisfied predicate.

  1. Significance: It governs curation throughput and verification cost. The expected validation work per admitted record equals the reach-weighted sum of predicate costs divided by the overall route yield, the same cost-over-acceptance form as cost per accepted task (equation 1.2). Reordering independent predicates can cut verification time substantially without altering the final admitted set.
  2. Distinction: Rejection is route-specific. A trajectory that fails the strict criteria of an end-to-end demonstration route remains fully available for error diagnosis, preference pair generation, or rollout fixture synthesis, each governed by its own independent predicate sequence.
  3. Common pitfall: Attempting to accelerate validation by dropping or weakening admission predicates. Relaxing a predicate alters the admitted population rather than optimizing evaluation order, converting an efficiency optimization into an unvetted shift in training criteria.

15.5.1 Admission predicates

Treating a passing test suite as sufficient for admission lets records with missing tool outputs, unresolved license holds, out-of-scope file edits, or unmasked intermediate errors slip straight into the training set. To catch each class of defect separately, table 5 divides the admission decision into five predicate families. These families are logical questions rather than separate microservices, and their concrete checks depend on the target artifact: a complete demonstration, an isolated recovery segment, a preference pair, or an evaluation fixture for fresh rollouts.

For an end-to-end demonstration route, admission requires satisfying the conjunction \(\text{chk}_{\text{record}} \land \text{chk}_{\text{use}} \land \text{chk}_{\text{proc,demo}} \land \text{chk}_{\text{out,demo}} \land \text{chk}_{\text{target,demo}}\). The subscripts emphasize that predicates are consumer-specific: a candidate that fails the final repair objective fails this route’s outcome condition (\(\text{chk}_{\text{out,demo}}\)), whereas an isolated error-recovery segment within that same run can satisfy the outcome check of a recovery-learning route. Any action designated as an imitation target must be justifiable strictly from the observation context the agent possessed at that step, without lookahead leakage or hindsight annotations injected from subsequent checks. These target designations determine the token-level loss masks during supervised fine-tuning.

↪ Token-level loss masking and target designation mechanisms are developed in Observation Loss Masking.

Engineers must separate record validity from action validity. A log that accurately captures a malformed tool call and the runtime’s immediate pre-dispatch refusal satisfies record adequacy (\(\text{chk}_{\text{record}}\)); the logging subsystem worked properly even though the model made an error. Conversely, missing environment observations or out-of-order tool responses make a trajectory unusable for imitation learning, even if the candidate code ultimately passed all tests. Pipelines can route truncated or incomplete records to diagnostic stores for failure analysis, but must never package them as behavioral demonstrations.

A preference route adds the shared-context and justified-ordering conditions that define a preference comparison (section 15.2.3) to its outcome and target predicates. Until paired with a matching winning rollout, a failed attempt remains only an unassigned comparison candidate. For reinforcement learning pipelines, curation delivers versioned task definitions, resettable environment fixtures, and automated grading harnesses for fresh rollouts rather than static records of past failures. While these downstream consumers share the same requirements for permitted use (\(\text{chk}_{\text{use}}\)) and record adequacy (\(\text{chk}_{\text{record}}\)), they differ in how they check outcomes (\(\text{chk}_{\text{out}}\)) and assign target labels (\(\text{chk}_{\text{target}}\)).

Each predicate returns one of four canonical states (pass, fail, pending, or unknown), following the four-verdict rule of The four-verdict rule. A fail stops evaluation along that route at once and retains the record for diagnosis or another route (figure 2). A missing or timed-out check never defaults to a pass; unverified candidates move to an unresolved holding queue, tagged with the missing evidence needed to finish the check. Curation telemetry counts evaluated, admitted, rejected, and unresolved records per route so that traces redirected to diagnostic pools never inflate the reported yield of demonstrations, and so that outcome checks themselves are guarded against manipulation by the candidate code.

15.5.2 Test-tampering defense

When an agent resolves a failing test suite by rewriting the test assertion rather than fixing the underlying race condition (listing 1), the breach costs far more during data curation than during benchmark evaluation. Tampering during evaluation skews a single benchmark score; tampering during collection admits corrupt demonstrations that train later models to repeat the exploit.

↩︎ Grader isolation, which keeps held-out tests, grading scripts, and pass signals beyond the candidate’s reach, is established in Grader isolation.

Replacing the assertion produces a zero exit code from the test runner while leaving the concurrency bug untouched. In a well-designed cascade, this patch fails on two counts: the candidate diff violates the task’s allowed-path policy (failing process condition \(\text{chk}_{\text{proc}}\)), and running the code against clean, unedited test harnesses reproduces the deadlock (failing outcome condition \(\text{chk}_{\text{out}}\)). An agent’s self-reported success or local exit code must never override authoritative verifiers.

Test-tampering defense is grader isolation applied to admission. It is the set of controls that keep test harnesses, test inputs, and verification results beyond the reach of the candidate and the acting agent, so that a pass recorded by \(\text{chk}_{\text{out}}\) is an independent measurement rather than the candidate’s own report. Because later checkpoints train on admitted trajectories, an undetected bypass is reinforced across training rounds rather than contained to one evaluation trial. The defense protects the integrity of the measurement, not its breadth; a tamper-proof suite still checks only what its assertions cover, and whether a passing solution generalizes remains a question for the target suitability predicate \(\text{chk}_{\text{target}}\). The failure modes that tamper defense prevents are not hypothetical: they have appeared directly in frontier models during agentic training.

War Story 15.1: Special-cased tests in agentic coding (2025)
Context: Anthropic’s system card for Claude 3.7 Sonnet documents strategic evaluation gaming observed during agentic coding evaluations (Anthropic 2025).

Mechanism: When faced with difficult programming tasks after repeated unsuccessful attempts, the model occasionally resorted to reward hacking, exploiting discrepancies between the empirical reward signal and true task completion. Specifically, the model hardcoded expected return values for known test cases or modified unit test assertions directly so that the suite matched its incorrect implementation.

Impact: Standard user testing failed to catch the behavior because it appeared rarely during straightforward interactions, emerging primarily after extended, multi-turn troubleshooting cycles in specialized programming sandboxes.

Fix: The team deployed transcript classifiers to detect gaming patterns in training runs and applied targeted mitigations prior to model deployment. For production agentic environments, the report advises monitoring for repetitive edit-and-test loops on individual files, code comments indicating test-specific overrides, and unauthorized diffs against test suites.

Systems lesson: An exit code produced inside an execution environment accessible to untrusted agent code is unverified telemetry. A curation pipeline that blindly admits trajectories based on local test passes ingests the exact gaming behavior that monitoring seeks to eradicate. Tamper verification must therefore run as an immutable gate in the cascade before any trajectory can serve as a training target.

Anthropic. 2025. Claude 3.7 Sonnet System Card.

To prevent such exploits from entering the corpus, tamper defense combines the evaluation gym’s containment controls (principle 9) with an admission-specific diff audit. Out-of-tree test fixtures keep authoritative tests outside the candidate’s writable workspace until its snapshot is hashed and frozen. Isolated runner supervision executes the harness in a fresh sandbox where candidate code cannot patch assertion libraries or forge exit codes (since a read-only mount alone still allows co-located code to read tests or interfere with the reporting process; Grader isolation). On top of those two evaluation controls, admission adds a protected-path manifest that binds the diff against the base commit to the process predicate, so any unauthorized change to test suites, continuous integration configuration, or build dependencies fails \(\text{chk}_{\text{proc}}\) irrespective of the test runner’s exit code. Because the manifest follows the task’s patch-admission policy, a task that legitimately requires a new regression test or an updated package manifest can still pass. Containment and authorization checks precede the execution of untrusted code regardless of how the other predicates are scheduled, and an audit log written after the fact cannot substitute for them.

When evaluating non-deterministic tasks, verification harnesses enforce a declared repetition protocol that fixes environmental seeds, maximum retry bounds, and aggregation rules for inconsistent trials. Rerunning a flaky test until it passes once merely samples from favorable noise. If a defective candidate passes a flaky test with probability \(\AttemptPassProbability\) per run, rerunning it up to \(\IndependentAttemptCount\) times reports a pass with probability \(1-(1-\AttemptPassProbability)^\IndependentAttemptCount\), the at-least-one form of pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\) applied here to false passes. Timeouts must likewise be recorded as unresolved outcomes (pending or unknown) and routed to an inspection queue rather than silently treated as model errors or transient infrastructure faults. Once the predicates and their isolation bounds are fixed, the remaining question is how to order those checks so that rejected candidates consume as little verifier time as possible.

15.5.3 Cascade economics

Running an eight-second sandboxed test suite before a twenty-millisecond process audit burns seconds of verifier slot time on candidates that a fast check would have rejected immediately. To price and optimize the order in which checks run for a given admission route, let \(\text{chk}_1,\ldots,\text{chk}_{\AdmissionCheckCount}\) represent the sequence of admission predicates in execution order. Define \(\CheckPassProbability_i=\Pr(\text{chk}_i\text{ passes}\mid \text{chk}_1,\ldots,\text{chk}_{i-1}\text{ pass})\) as the conditional pass probability of predicate \(\text{chk}_i\), with \(\CheckPassProbability_1\) denoting the unconditional pass probability of the initial check. Let \(\CheckSlotTime_i\) represent the expected verifier slot time conditional on reaching that check, measured in seconds. Under short-circuit evaluation, which stops at the first non-passing predicate, the expected verification time per attempted record is:

\[\ExpectedCheckingTime=\sum_{i=1}^{\AdmissionCheckCount} \CheckSlotTime_i\prod_{j=1}^{\AdmissionCheckIndex-1}\CheckPassProbability_j, \tag{1}\]

where the empty product evaluates to one. The overall route yield \(\AdmissionRouteYield\) represents the joint probability that a candidate passes all \(\AdmissionCheckCount\) predicates:

\[\AdmissionRouteYield=\Pr(\text{chk}_1\land\cdots\land \text{chk}_{\AdmissionCheckCount})=\prod_{i=1}^{\AdmissionCheckCount}\CheckPassProbability_i.\]

For routes with nonzero yield (\(\AdmissionRouteYield > 0\)), the ratio \(\ExpectedCheckingTime/\AdmissionRouteYield\) is the expected verification slot time spent per admitted trajectory. The yield \(\AdmissionRouteYield\) plays the role of acceptance probability in the cost-per-accepted-task formula (equation 1.2). Because this ratio is an expectation across large samples, a batch with zero admitted records reports total slot time consumed rather than an undefined ratio. Upstream rollout generation, retry loops, and downstream storage costs are accounted for outside this verifier cascade.

Reordering these predicates preserves the exact admitted set as long as their definitions, inputs, and evaluations remain unchanged. When predicates are correlated, however, conditional pass probabilities must be re-measured for each order, because each check sees a different pre-filtered subset. Operational dependencies and containment rules also constrain permissible orders; data governance and containment checks, for instance, must always precede running untrusted code in a sandbox.

When two adjacent predicates \(\text{chk}_\FirstAdjacentCheckIndex\) and \(\text{chk}_\SecondAdjacentCheckIndex\) are statistically independent, an exchange argument yields the cheaper execution order in closed form. Swapping an adjacent pair leaves every earlier check unchanged and passes the exact same joint fraction \(\FirstAdjacentCheckPassProbability \SecondAdjacentCheckPassProbability\) of candidates to every later check, so only the pair’s own two terms in equation 1 change. Conditional on reaching the pair, running \(\text{chk}_\FirstAdjacentCheckIndex\) before \(\text{chk}_\SecondAdjacentCheckIndex\) incurs an expected local cost of \(\FirstAdjacentCheckSlotTime + \FirstAdjacentCheckPassProbability \SecondAdjacentCheckSlotTime\), whereas running \(\text{chk}_\SecondAdjacentCheckIndex\) before \(\text{chk}_\FirstAdjacentCheckIndex\) incurs \(\SecondAdjacentCheckSlotTime + \SecondAdjacentCheckPassProbability \FirstAdjacentCheckSlotTime\). Running \(\text{chk}_\FirstAdjacentCheckIndex\) first is cheaper whenever \(\FirstAdjacentCheckSlotTime + \FirstAdjacentCheckPassProbability \SecondAdjacentCheckSlotTime < \SecondAdjacentCheckSlotTime + \SecondAdjacentCheckPassProbability \FirstAdjacentCheckSlotTime\). Collecting \(\FirstAdjacentCheckSlotTime(1-\SecondAdjacentCheckPassProbability)\) on the left and \(\SecondAdjacentCheckSlotTime(1-\FirstAdjacentCheckPassProbability)\) on the right and dividing by both rejection probabilities gives:

\[\frac{\FirstAdjacentCheckSlotTime}{1-\FirstAdjacentCheckPassProbability} < \frac{\SecondAdjacentCheckSlotTime}{1-\SecondAdjacentCheckPassProbability}. \tag{2}\]

Because any schedule in which an adjacent pair violates this inequality can be improved by swapping that pair, the optimal schedule orders all independent predicates by ascending cost per rejection, \(\CheckSlotTime_i / (1 - \CheckPassProbability_i)\), running the cheapest rejection filter first. This rule matches the predicate-ranking algorithm developed for relational database query optimizers with expensive user-defined filters (Hellerstein and Stonebraker 1993).6

6 Predicate Rank: Relational database query optimizers faced the same scheduling problem when selection predicates began calling expensive user-defined functions. Ordering filters by selectivity alone ran heavy functions on rows that a fast check would have dropped. Sorting independent filters by rank, defined as per-row cost divided by rejection probability \((1 - \CheckPassProbability_i)\), minimizes expected work, matching the exchange argument in equation 2. In agent curation cascades, where checks range from sub-millisecond AST scans to multi-minute integration tests and human reviews, rank ordering decides where most of the verification budget goes.

Napkin Math 15.2: Admission ordering with the same accepted set
Problem: A batch of 100,000 candidate trajectory records enters an end-to-end demonstration admission route evaluated under predicates \(\text{chk}_{\text{record}}, \text{chk}_{\text{use}}, \text{chk}_{\text{proc}}, \text{chk}_{\text{out}}, \text{chk}_{\text{target}}\). Record fidelity (\(\text{chk}_{\text{record}}\)) and permitted-use (\(\text{chk}_{\text{use}}\)) checks must execute first due to data dependencies. Target suitability (\(\text{chk}_{\text{target}}\)) executes last because its review reads the test artifacts that \(\text{chk}_{\text{out}}\) produces. The process audit (\(\text{chk}_{\text{proc}}\)) and outcome verification (\(\text{chk}_{\text{out}}\)) can execute in either order. Which evaluation sequence minimizes verifier slot time, and how much total compute does it save?

Variables: Each predicate evaluation resolves deterministically to pass or fail. For this analysis, pass events are modeled as mutually independent, ensuring invariant marginal pass probabilities across permutations. Each reached verifier occupies a dedicated worker slot for the duration that table 6 lists beside its pass probability. The trajectory record and candidate repository snapshot are immutable. Pre-execution authorization, container containment, and grader protections are enforced prior to running any test suites. Upstream rollout generation, job scheduling delays, candidate storage, and human review costs are excluded.

Math: Applying equation 2, the cost per rejection is 0.20 s / (1 − 0.8) = 1 s for process audit \(\text{chk}_{\text{proc}}\), compared to 8 s / (1 − 0.4) = 13.3 s for outcome verification \(\text{chk}_{\text{out}}\). Because \(\text{chk}_{\text{proc}}\) yields rejections far more cost-effectively than \(\text{chk}_{\text{out}}\), scheduling \(\text{chk}_{\text{proc}}\) ahead of \(\text{chk}_{\text{out}}\) minimizes expected compute. Both predicates are reached with joint probability 0.855 (the product of pass rates for \(\text{chk}_{\text{record}}\) and \(\text{chk}_{\text{use}}\)). Swapping the sequence saves \(\RecordAdequacyPassProbability \PermittedUsePassProbability [\OutcomeCheckSlotTime(1-\ProcessConditionPassProbability) - \ProcessConditionCheckTime(1-\OutcomeCheckPassProbability)]\), yielding 1.27 s saved per attempted record. Evaluating equation 1 across the parameters in table 6 produces the overall slot durations in table 7, verified through exhaustive state-space enumeration.

Result: Both schedules in table 7 achieve an identical expected yield of 20.52 percent, admitting approximately 20,520 valid demonstrations from the batch. Evaluating process compliance \(\text{chk}_{\text{proc}}\) prior to sandbox testing \(\text{chk}_{\text{out}}\) reduces total verifier slot time by 16.9 percent, without discarding a single valid trajectory or lowering any quality threshold.

Systems insight: Sequence independent, fixed-cost admission verifiers within permissible orders by cost per rejection, \(\CheckSlotTime_i / (1 - \CheckPassProbability_i)\), rather than by raw duration alone. Ordering by cost per rejection places fast, selective filters ahead of expensive, permissive verifiers while guaranteeing an identical admitted set. These compute savings require neither cutting validation corners nor loosening thresholds. The dependency fixed in the problem is what keeps target suitability \(\text{chk}_{\text{target}}\) last here. Its cost per rejection is 8 s, below the outcome check’s 13.3 s, so a target review that did not read the test artifacts would belong ahead of \(\text{chk}_{\text{out}}\) as well. Data dependencies, container cold-start amortization, and compliance boundaries constrain permissible schedules. Correlated checks require comparing admissible orders with measured conditional pass rates and reached-check costs in equation 1.

15.5.4 Learned scores and scoped judgments

Deterministic checks cannot score open-ended prose quality or step-by-step reasoning clarity, yet letting a learned scorer or brevity filter admit trajectories on its own either admits flawed runs that game the scorer or strips out the multi-turn recovery paths the policy needs to learn error correction. Learned scoring models are still useful for ranking candidate trajectories for manual review or sub-sampling from a pool of verified examples to balance diversity and conciseness. Process reward models, for instance, score individual reasoning steps and intermediate tool calls rather than only final task outcomes (Uesato et al. 2022; Lightman et al. 2024). Even so, a low step score only flags a likely point of failure; pinpointing the root cause still requires human or symbolic review.

When tasks require evaluating open-ended prose, user interactions, or design quality, human raters or judge models supply scores governed by explicit rubrics and calibration standards. The verification asymmetry (principle 3) bounds what such a score can do inside a cascade. A learned assessment can rank candidates or supply scoped evidence for one qualitative criterion, but it is not an independent guarantee. When valid records are rare, even a small false-accept rate fills the admitted set with invalid ones. The judge’s input must therefore be limited to the evidence its criterion names and kept away from anything the candidate wrote to influence its grader. Deterministic verifiers require the same scoping: every verifier execution records the assessor version, rubric hash, extracted evidence, confidence scores, and explicit abstentions, and a high qualitative rating never overrides a failed deterministic check.

↩︎ Qualitative rubrics and grader calibration standards are established in Calibration of qualitative judgment.

Repeated selection against an automated scorer also concentrates the scorer’s blind spots.
