Filtering training data against a learned proxy score initially improves output quality, but continued optimization eventually triggers proxy collapse, where the proxy score keeps rising while true downstream task quality falls (Gao et al. 2023). This is the same divergence that best-of-N selection produces against an imperfect check at inference time (How search exploits imperfect checks), except that curation writes the winners into the next policy’s weights. Mitigating this feedback loop requires periodic double-blind audits of both accepted and rejected records, continuous recalibration against held-out ground truth that never enters training, and re-scoring whenever policy architectures or task domains shift. Any data augmentation or trajectory transformation likewise requires fresh verification of its modified payload and token masks, a provenance boundary that section 15.9 develops.

Checkpoint 15.1: Defend an admission route

A route is defensible when each predicate’s verdict and the order of checking can both be justified.

Deciding admission

Pricing the cascade

By evaluating trajectories through staged, consumer-specific cascades rather than forcing all data through a single cleanliness filter, the curation pipeline produces verified datasets tailored to distinct imitation, preference, and rollout objectives. Verifying clean executions, however, leaves the harder half of the data problem unsolved: production agents encounter disruptions, and training a policy to recover requires curating traces that contain mistakes without teaching the model to repeat them.

15.6 Recovery Demonstration Curation

When an agent issues a malformed tool call, receives a rejection from the environment runtime, and then inspects the schema to produce a passing patch, its trace records both a mistake and a valid repair. A naive curation pipeline might discard this trace because it contains a failed step, or edit out the error to make the agent appear infallible. Either choice discards the evidence of how to recover from an imperfect prefix. Admitting the successful repair while keeping the original misstep as conditioning context requires deciding which recovery trajectories to harvest, how to mask them safely, and how much training capacity to give them.

A training corpus composed solely of error-free trajectories teaches an agent how to act only when every prior step succeeded. In production, however, noisy tool outputs, transient network faults, and early mistakes push an agent off the nominal path. Once off that path, a model trained only on clean prefixes faces states it never saw during training, and over a long horizon a recoverable detour turns into a failed task. This distribution mismatch7 creates the need for recovery demonstrations, and the curation pipeline’s job is to capture, validate, and package those recovery episodes with their conditioning evidence intact.

7 Compounding Imitation Error: A policy trained only on states visited by an expert can turn a per-step error rate \(\PerStepErrorRate\) (the per-step error rate of horizon compounding, equation 1.1) into as many as \(\TurnHorizon^2\PerStepErrorRate\) mistakes over an \(\TurnHorizon\)-step task, because an early error exposes the policy to states absent from its training distribution. Querying an expert interactively on learner-visited states, the core mechanism of DAgger, restores an error bound that grows linearly in \(\TurnHorizon\) (Ross et al. 2011). Recovery demonstrations are the offline curation counterpart: they supply supervised repair targets on the flawed state prefixes that direct-success collection omits.

Ross, Stéphane, Geoffrey Gordon, and J. Andrew Bagnell. 2011. “A Reduction of Imitation Learning and Structured Prediction to No-Regret Online Learning.” Proceedings of the Fourteenth International Conference on Artificial Intelligence and Statistics (AISTATS) 15: 627–35.

↪ Exposure bias, the mismatch between training on recorded prefixes and running on prefixes the policy produced itself, and its horizon-dependent cost are developed in Exposure Bias.

Definition 15.2: Recovery demonstration

A recovery demonstration is a curated trajectory record or isolated sequence segment that pairs the context of an operational disruption (a model mistake, environment fault, or partial observation) with an action assessed as correct under the task specification and runtime authority boundaries.

  1. Significance: Recovery demonstrations directly address long-horizon execution stability. Under horizon compounding (equation 1.1), any step-level error that the policy can successfully remediate becomes a temporary detour rather than an irrecoverable task failure. Direct-success harvesting systematically starves the training distribution of these pivotal recovery branches.
  2. Distinction: An appropriate recovery action depends on context: it may diagnose an exception, retry with corrected parameters, back off, or hand off control to a human supervisor. It does not require the agent to overcome an outage it cannot repair or to exceed its authority to finish the task.
  3. Common pitfall: Treating every token in an eventually successful trajectory as a positive imitation target, thereby inadvertently teaching the model to repeat the initial mistake.

15.6.1 Record roles and learning uses

A single extended trajectory may begin with clean progress, hit an unexpected environment error, execute a sound recovery, and later stall on an unrelated tool failure. Discarding that entire trajectory wastes genuine recovery signal, whereas training uncritically on all of its tokens teaches the model to replicate its bugs. Because a trace rarely fits a single outcome bucket, labels such as direct success, recovery, and negative describe the behavioral evidence a segment contains rather than granting blanket permission for training. The predicates of table 5 already separate the two decisions. Record adequacy and permitted use are shared across routes, while the process, outcome, and target predicates are set by the consumer. A role label therefore indicates which consumer-specific checks a segment might pass (table 8).

A hard negative is an action or plan that looks plausible under surface checks, such as valid syntax, expected keywords, or a passing local smoke test, yet fails full functional verification. Pinpointing the exact step where an agentic trace went wrong is rarely simple. A failed assertion at the end of a run confirms that an error occurred without naming the earlier decision that caused it. Repeated tool calls that look like a loop may be legitimate polling against an asynchronous job. Curation systems therefore record the failure signal that implicates a candidate divergence step together with an explicit note when the first causal step is uncertain, rather than inventing an exact divergence point. Engineering teams also frequently assume that any recorded negative can be paired immediately with a success to train a preference objective, or replayed as a baseline for reinforcement learning against a verifiable reward. Both shortcuts fail in practice. A negative forms a valid comparison only under the shared-context condition of section 15.2.3, which a success generated under a different prompt, an extra hint, or updated tool outputs violates. A reinforcement learning baseline, in turn, needs a live, resettable fixture rather than a stored failure transcript.

Systems Perspective 15.1: A recorded mistake is context, not a target
A pervasive misconception in trajectory engineering is that an imitation training set must consist exclusively of pristine, error-free transcripts. In reality, a curated training dataset consists of complete conversational contexts coupled with explicit target masks, and these two components serve fundamentally different purposes.

A flawed model response should receive no direct positive imitation target loss. Nevertheless, that erroneous response must remain in the prompt history if subsequent recovery actions are to make any sense; a correction is meaningful only in the context of the error it resolves. Conversely, an execution trace that ultimately timed out or failed an integration test often contains early sub-tasks solved with exemplary precision. The curation pipeline marks which spans represent valid targets and which represent conditioning context; the loss-masking subsystem then omits their direct next-token loss terms while retaining them as conditioning during fine-tuning. Masking errors out of the loss while retaining them in the attention context teaches the model how to react to a failure without training it to generate one.

15.6.2 Conditioning evidence for a correction

In collection run collection_run_r19_01, target selection separates context from targets on task pool_repair_01 inside test environment pool_fixture_v1. The run produces workspace candidate ws_r20 and captures raw trace trace_r19_01 (workspace numbers count candidate revisions after the evaluation trial’s ws_r19, while run and trace numbers count collection batches). End-to-end grading confirms that ws_r20 satisfies repair_accept_v1, meeting all process constraints, resource ceilings, and cleanup duties.

The run belongs to a campaign whose declared learning use is recovery from harness refusals. Unlike the truncated observation that opened the chapter, this behavior is collected on the premise, recorded in the collection manifest, that the ladder’s probes left it with the model. Supplying the schema in context and validating proposals against a strict schema still left the policy emitting malformed edits that the runtime had to refuse. What remains to teach is how to respond after a refusal, and the recorded event sequence shows how that recovery unfolds:

  • Event e1 delivers the task prompt, tool definitions, and repository context.
  • At event e2, the model proposes a file edit with a malformed argument. Because the tool runtime validates inputs locally before dispatch, it refuses the call immediately, and the trace records the refusal without logging an applied write.
  • At event e3, the model reads the refusal, requests the API schema documentation, and inspects the returned specification.
  • At event e4, the model issues a corrected, schema-compliant edit, and the tool enforces an atomic version check alongside the update rather than relying on a stale earlier read.
  • At event e5, the harness runs the required checks on the edited workspace and returns their summary as an observation.
  • At event e6, the model summarizes the validated changes, states the patch scope, and closes the session.

Under curation policy repair_accept_v1, an initial syntax refusal that the agent diagnoses and corrects promptly is eligible for recovery training. A target-selection manifest marks the actions worth imitating without editing the recorded sequence of events:

{
  "collection_run_id": "collection_run_r19_01",
  "source_trace_id": "trace_r19_01",
  "criteria_version": "repair_accept_v1",
  "target_selection_version": "recovery_targets_v1",
  "behavioral_role": "recovery",
  "context_only_events": ["e2", "e5"],
  "selected_response_events": ["e3", "e4", "e6"],
  "assessment_scope": "recorded correction and supported final report"
}

The source trace preserves the original task prompt, tool definitions, intermediate observations, and evaluation logs intact, while target selection marks only the policy tokens inside events e3, e4, and e6. The refused proposal (e2) and test summary (e5) stay in the sequence as conditioning context, supplying the history that makes the schema lookup and corrected edit make sense. Passing the overall task does not make every intermediate action a training target; each selected step must be a sound decision given what the agent knew at that turn. Placed beside the other two record roles, the recovery record sits between a direct success, whose supported responses can all be targets, and a hard negative, whose deficient response is never one (figure 3).

When only a local recovery window has verified evidence, the curation harness can admit that segment alone while leaving the broader task outcome marked as unverified. Likewise, if post-processing scrubs credentials or private paths to create trace_r19_01_redacted_v1, the pipeline must re-verify the target spans against the modified tokens. Deleting the malformed call, pasting future test outputs into an earlier prompt, or truncating intermediate observations changes the causal problem the model is being asked to learn. Preserving those causal links inside harvested traces is only half of recovery curation, however, because waiting passively for natural failures leaves rare fault modes unrepresented.

15.6.3 Fault injection

In production, routine execution occasionally exposes an agent to network drops, service timeouts, and tool errors, but waiting for spontaneous failures leaves wide gaps in the training corpus. Systematic fault injection8 fills those gaps deliberately. A collection protocol logs the injection version, the targeted tool call or observation stream, the simulated system state, and the expected recovery action. Fault injection stays strictly inside the runtime’s security boundaries: injecting a network timeout or file lock grants the agent no extra authority to install unapproved packages, widen filesystem permissions, or bypass human approval gates. Each condition in table 9 names the causal distinction the fixture must preserve and the response a curator checks.

8 Fault Injection: Introducing controlled failures to test resilience is a classic systems practice, popularized in distributed infrastructure as chaos engineering, which injects server, network, and disk faults into live clusters to check steady-state behavior (Basiri et al. 2016). Curation borrows the experimental discipline rather than the live production venue: faults are injected inside versioned, hermetic test fixtures where every fault is logged and environment state transitions stay deterministic, so demonstrations teach genuine recovery rather than quirks of the simulator.

Basiri, A., N. Behnam, R. de Rooij, L. Hochstein, L. Kosewski, J. Reynolds, and C. Rosenthal. 2016. “Chaos Engineering.” IEEE Software 33 (3): 35–41. https://doi.org/10.1109/ms.2016.60.

Consider the lost-acknowledgment case in table 9. When a mock harness drops an acknowledgment after committing a state-changing remote call, the simulated environment must keep the side effects of that committed call in its state. If the mock returned a connection reset while silently rolling the server state back to a clean slate, it would misrepresent the distributed systems hazard and train the agent to retry blindly, causing duplicate writes in production. Replay determinism requires that every subsequent observation reflect the mutations that actually occurred.

Engineering teams must also separate tasks meant to teach autonomous repair from tasks meant to teach safe stopping. A recovery task provides an authorized, reachable fix inside the test environment; a safe-stopping task deliberately closes every repair path so the agent learns to halt cleanly and hand off to an operator. Because the two objectives require different grading rubrics, reliability coverage tracks unprompted agent mistakes and injected faults separately while recording any production failure modes that the fixture cannot reproduce. Harvesting these multi-turn recovery traces, however, changes the token geometry of the resulting corpus.

15.6.4 Records, stored tokens, and selected targets

Because a recovery trajectory includes diagnostic cycles, failed attempts, and error observations, it is much longer than a direct-success run, yet only the corrected responses carry supervised loss. As a result, the share of recovery trajectories in a corpus matches neither their share of stored tokens on disk nor their share of active target tokens during training. Curation accounting starts from a retained pool of trajectories and draws subsets for specific training artifacts. One retained trace may supply conditioning context and target spans to an imitation corpus while also entering a preference pair, yet it remains a single task execution in the pool.

One recovery class, three shares: records, stored tokens, targets.

For a trajectory category \(\TrajectoryCategoryIndex\), let \(\CategoryRecordCount_j\) denote the number of retained records, \(\MeanStoredTokensPerRecord^{(\TrajectoryCategoryIndex)}\) denote the average stored tokens per record under the active tokenizer, and \(\MeanTargetTokensPerRecord_j\) denote the average selected target tokens per record admitted into a specific imitation fine-tuning artifact. The total stored token volume \(\StoredTokenVolume_j\) and total target token volume \(\TargetTokenVolume_j\) are:

\[\StoredTokenVolume_j=\CategoryRecordCount_j\MeanStoredTokensPerRecord^{(\TrajectoryCategoryIndex)},\qquad \TargetTokenVolume_j=\CategoryRecordCount_j\MeanTargetTokensPerRecord_j.\]

The stored token share measures \(\StoredTokenVolume_j\) against the total tokens in the repository, whereas the target token share measures \(\TargetTokenVolume_j\) against the active supervised tokens in the training dataset. Confusing the two breaks capacity planning. Processed tokens (context plus targets) drive attention compute and memory traffic during forward and backward passes. Selected target tokens, by contrast, decide which positions contribute direct loss terms (whose gradients still flow back through the conditioning context). Specifying a dataset mixture by record count while budgeting accelerators by target tokens, or vice versa, creates scheduling surprises, especially when multi-turn conversations are unrolled without prefix sharing.

Napkin Math 15.3: One pool, three different token allocations
Problem: Consider a hypothetical retained corpus containing 50,000 records categorized into disjoint roles. The demonstration policy admits direct successes and recovery trajectories; negative trajectories are reserved for diagnostic logging and preference modeling, providing zero imitation targets for this artifact. Using the empirical means listed in table 10, determine the fractional share that recovery trajectories occupy across three distinct denominators: total records, tokens (stored in the repository and processed by this artifact), and supervised target tokens.

Math: Total stored tokens follow \(\StoredTokenVolume_j = \CategoryRecordCount_j\MeanStoredTokensPerRecord^{(\TrajectoryCategoryIndex)}\): direct successes account for 234,000,000, recovery traces account for 231,250,000, and negatives account for 49,000,000, totaling 514,250,000 across the repository and 465,250,000 across admitted demonstration records. Supervised target tokens follow \(\TargetTokenVolume_j = \CategoryRecordCount_j\MeanTargetTokensPerRecord_j\): direct demonstrations contribute 39,000,000 target tokens and recovery traces contribute 25,000,000 target tokens, totaling 64,000,000 supervisory tokens.

Result: Recovery accounts for 25 percent of total retained records, 44.97 percent of all stored tokens in the repository, 49.70 percent of the admitted demonstration tokens, and 39.06 percent of active target supervision tokens. The two token shares answer different questions. The repository share includes negatives that this artifact never trains on, so it prices storage; the admitted-demonstration share counts the tokens that training actually processes, so it prices compute.

Systems insight: Always formulate training mixtures in terms of target tokens while budgeting accelerator compute and memory in terms of processed context tokens. The operational multiplier separating these two metrics is the context-to-target ratio: 9.25 stored context tokens per target token for recovery traces, compared to 6 for direct successes. Increasing the recovery proportion inflates processed context volume far faster than supervised gradient signal. An engineering team that configures a mixture purely by record count will request a 25 percent recovery ratio but will end up committing 44.97 percent of repository storage and 49.70 percent of processed training tokens to recovery records. Naive multi-turn serialization that repeatedly unrolls conversational prefixes amplifies this computational overhead even further.

15.6.5 Mixture experiments

Token accounting prices the storage and compute footprint of a recovery-heavy corpus, but it cannot tell whether adding more recovery traces actually improves fault tolerance or crowds out direct-path competence on routine tasks. Answering that question requires an empirical mixture experiment that holds the evaluation protocol, base checkpoint, target-masking rules, and training schedule fixed while varying the recovery ratio across training cohorts. Curation engineers track deduplication and replacement in each cohort, because raising the recovery share under a fixed training budget reduces coverage of direct-path tasks.

Because context-to-target ratios differ by role, an experiment must state which denominator it holds constant and report results under both. Fixing total supervised target tokens forces the recovery-heavy cohort to process far more conditioning context tokens, raising training wall-clock time and FLOPs; fixing total processed tokens gives the recovery-heavy cohort fewer supervised target updates. When accelerator hours or training windows are hard limits, teams compare candidate mixtures at equal wall-clock time or compute cost. They screen those candidates on the development split and test the chosen configuration once on the held-out evaluation benchmark. To keep aggregate scores from hiding regressions, evaluation reports break results down across nominal tasks, injected fault conditions, and out-of-distribution transfer suites before computing any summary metric. They track task acceptance alongside hallucinated tool outputs, redundant steps, clean stops, and total token spend.

Once an effective mixture is chosen, its parameters are written into the corpus manifest, the machine-readable, versioned record that publishes a corpus and tells a trainer what each member is and how it may be used.9 Beyond the mixture weights, the manifest binds input trajectory pool identifiers, selection filter versions, token accounting denominators, test coverage matrices, and audit logs, whose per-record lineage and publication are developed in section 15.9 and section 15.10.2. Deploying a model trained on this manifest changes the trajectories that subsequent collection runs produce. New failure modes and recoveries feed back into the harvesting pipeline, which closes a loop in which the policy being improved also generates the data that improves it.

9 Dataset Documentation: Documenting datasets with structured records that state how they were built, what they contain, and what uses they support was proposed by analogy to hardware datasheets in electronics (Gebru et al. 2021). A corpus manifest turns that documentation into a machine-checked contract for agent pipelines: its fields bind cryptographically to dataset versions, filter code, and verification logs so that training jobs can check corpus invariants automatically before starting a run.

Gebru, Timnit, Jamie Morgenstern, Briana Vecchione, Jennifer Wortman Vaughan, Hanna Wallach, Hal Daumé III, and Kate Crawford. 2021. “Datasheets for Datasets.” Communications of the ACM 64 (12): 86–92. https://doi.org/10.1145/3458723.

15.7 The Self-Improvement Loop

Suppose a curation round for the connection-pool repair agent retains only straight-line, clean executions while discarding messier traces where the agent recovers from misleading test diagnostics. Fine-tuning on that corpus strengthens straightforward repairs while leaving recovery unimproved, and in the next round a higher nominal pass rate hides that gap rather than closing it. Because corpus selection shapes the behaviors the next policy generation learns and samples, curators track three distinct signals in every round: what the current policy sampled, what the curation cascade kept, and how the resulting model scores under independent held-out evaluation.

15.7.1 Rejection-sampling fine-tuning

When a team lacks human demonstrations for new task fixtures, it can bootstrap training data from the current policy whenever verifying a candidate solution is easier than generating one, provided it logs attempted, rejected, and unverified runs alongside retained examples so a looser filter is never mistaken for a better model. In this generate–select–train loop, a versioned policy samples executions across a suite of task fixtures within a fixed compute budget. The admission cascade routes the resulting traces by role: clean demonstrations feed imitation buffers, branched pairs populate preference datasets, and unresolved traces enter diagnostic storage. Training on the admitted records produces a candidate checkpoint, which held-out evaluation tests to decide whether it becomes the generator for the next collection round (an internal development promotion that stays separate from production release).

Selecting sampled successes for imitation is rejection-sampling fine-tuning.10 The Self-Taught Reasoner (STaR) illustrates this loop in bootstrapped reasoning. The model generates candidate rationales, keeps those that reach correct answers, fine-tunes on them, and uses ground-truth answers to prompt new rationales on tasks it initially failed (Zelikman et al. 2022). In agentic tasks, an automated verifier checks that a run satisfies its acceptance criteria, while target review still checks that the intermediate tool calls and state transitions are sound enough to imitate.

10 Expert Iteration: The generate–select–train loop predates language models. In expert iteration, tree search guided by the current network finds stronger moves than the network alone, the network is trained to imitate them, and the updated network guides the next round of search (Anthony et al. 2017). Rejection-sampling fine-tuning replaces tree search with repeated sampling and the expert with an automated check, which is why any blind spot in the check quickly becomes a habit of the loop.

Anthony, Thomas, Zheng Tian, and David Barber. 2017. “Thinking Fast and Slow with Deep Learning and Tree Search.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 30.

Different learning objectives need different outcome mixes across attempts on a fixture. An all-success batch supplies valid imitation demonstrations showing clean paths through tools. An all-failure batch yields no end-to-end demonstrations under a success filter, yet still supplies localized recovery segments, negative candidates for preference pairs, and failure traces for new regression fixtures. Group-relative training objectives, by contrast, require outcome variation within the same fixture. They score each rollout by its reward minus the mean reward of the rollouts sampled for that fixture, so a batch in which every rollout gets the same reward produces zero advantage on every rollout and contributes no reward-surrogate gradient.

↪ Group relative policy optimization, which scores each rollout against the other rollouts sampled for the same task, is developed in Group Relative Policy Optimization.

The sampling budget required to harvest these different data types follows from the per-attempt pass rate. Suppose the collection policy succeeds on a fixture with probability \(\AttemptPassProbability\) per attempt, with each of the \(\IndependentAttemptCount\) attempts executed independently. A simple success filter extracts a demonstration from the fixture if at least one attempt succeeds, an event that occurs with probability \(1-(1-\AttemptPassProbability)^\IndependentAttemptCount\). This expected yield sets the baseline compute cost per retained trajectory when the collector keeps at most one success from each full batch. For preference comparisons based on binary task acceptance and group-relative objectives with binary rewards, useful within-group contrast requires at least one success and at least one failure. Subtracting both the all-failure probability \((1-\AttemptPassProbability)^\IndependentAttemptCount\) and the all-success probability \(\AttemptPassProbability^{\IndependentAttemptCount}\) yields the informative-task fraction:

\[\InformativeTaskFraction(\AttemptPassProbability,\IndependentAttemptCount)=1-(1-\AttemptPassProbability)^\IndependentAttemptCount-\AttemptPassProbability^{\IndependentAttemptCount}. \tag{3}\]

With 8 attempts, a fixture the policy solves 5 percent of the time yields a success with probability only 0.34, requiring an average of about 24 attempts to harvest a single successful trajectory. That same fixture forms a mixed group of successes and failures with probability 0.34. At 50 percent, where the task is moderately difficult, the mixed fraction reaches 0.99, providing abundant pairwise contrast. When the pass rate reaches 95 percent, nearly every fixture yields a success, but the mixed fraction drops back to 0.34 because batches rarely include a failing attempt. Equation 3 measures contrast rather than general utility. All-success groups remain valid demonstrations for imitation learning. By contrast, Zero-contrast groups applies the \(\AttemptPassProbability^{\IndependentAttemptCount}\) and \((1-\AttemptPassProbability)^\IndependentAttemptCount\) terms to isolate groups whose uniform rewards offer zero reward-surrogate gradient in group-relative optimization.

Easy fixtures yield successes most often; mid-difficulty fixtures yield contrast most often.

Choosing fixture difficulty is therefore a resource allocation decision. Hard fixtures cost about 24 attempts per harvested success, but they force the agent into unfamiliar states and expose missing recovery skills. Easy fixtures cost almost nothing per success, serving as cheap regression anchors that preserve baseline competence. For rare or high-consequence task families, low harvest yields are a worthwhile price for keeping coverage intact. Recording the sampling and retention policies alongside the corpus makes these shifts visible across retraining rounds, where evolving interfaces can invalidate older records and repeated self-selection can narrow behavioral coverage.

15.7.2 Staleness and coverage narrowing

Across those retraining rounds, a trajectory recorded minutes ago is already unusable if it called a deprecated tool schema or passed a flawed grader, whereas a trace recorded months ago by an older policy remains a valid demonstration as long as the tools and environment semantics have not changed. In an evolving agent system, staleness therefore measures architectural incompatibility rather than chronological age. Trajectory stores prevent silent corruption by binding four provenance fields to every record: the generating policy, the environment fixture, the tool interface schema, and the grading criteria. When grading criteria change, the pipeline re-runs verification. When tool schemas change, existing records undergo schema migration and revalidation. As the policy improves, sampling shifts toward the harder edge cases the updated agent still fails.

Even when every schema and grader stays current, repeated self-training narrows behavioral coverage. Because strategies that cleared earlier filters appear more often in fine-tuning data, the updated policy reproduces those paths more often and samples them even more heavily in the next round. Left unchecked, training repeatedly on a model’s own filtered outputs shrinks distributional diversity, a form of model collapse (Shumailov et al. 2024) driven by the hidden feedback loop between a model’s outputs and its future training set (Sculley et al. 2015). Automated verifiers reject failures covered by their checks, but filtering alone cannot break that self-reinforcing loop; teams must measure and bound the drift directly.

Three telemetry signals warn when coverage is narrowing across rounds. Within each fixture, the number of distinct normalized action sequences falls as the policy repeats the same routines; across attempts on a fixture, pass@1 continues to rise while pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\) at large \(\IndependentAttemptCount\) flattens out, showing that probability mass is concentrating on familiar paths rather than discovering alternative solutions; and across the benchmark, scores on held-out task families stall even as acceptance on training fixtures climbs. Because a drop in distinct action sequences can simply mean the agent is cutting out unneeded retries, engineers read action diversity alongside held-out transfer scores: when shrinking diversity coincides with flat held-out performance, the policy is over-specializing.

↩︎ pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\), the probability that at least one of \(\IndependentAttemptCount\) sampled candidates passes, is established in Candidate availability under pass@\IndependentAttemptCount.

Countering this narrowing requires bounding how heavily any single fixture or synthetic round can shape the dataset. At the fixture level, per-task retention caps keep easily solved problems from flooding the corpus, and deduplication filters drop identical normalized action sequences unless they occurred under genuinely different observations. Across retraining rounds, the corpus pairs synthetic runs with a permanent anchor of verified external demonstrations (such as curated human traces) that self-play cannot overwrite. Early-stopping rules halt the loop as soon as action diversity and held-out scores plateau, even if training-fixture acceptance keeps rising.

External demonstrations and held-out evaluation suites play distinct roles here. Curated human or expert demonstrations anchor the training mixture because they come from outside the current policy’s habits. Protected evaluation tasks, by contrast, stay outside both training and automated selection under the development and final-test split discipline of section 15.4. Because each promotion step in a self-improvement loop consults a holdout score, the benchmark that gates round-by-round promotion acts as a development set over multiple rounds, and a separate final-test suite must verify any final generalization claim. Periodic human audits across accepted, rejected, and borderline runs catch grader shortcuts and missed edge cases before promotion, while production telemetry supplies fresh task candidates from live traffic.

15.7.3 Production feedback signals

Production sessions expose edge cases that offline fixtures never anticipated, but treating raw user actions as ground-truth labels corrupts a corpus. A user who accepts a patch without editing it may never have run the tests, and a user who abandons a session may have been interrupted. Extracting training signals from live telemetry requires treating each observed event as evidence of the user’s immediate action rather than a verdict on task correctness (table 11).

Misreading these signals distorts the training set. When a user edits an agent’s output to add a new requirement, the edited text answers the revised request rather than the original prompt; training on that edited output without updating the prompt teaches the model to guess unstated constraints. Likewise, pairing an agent’s draft with a user’s edit as a preference comparison is valid only when the context and task goal stay identical across both versions. Telemetry pipelines therefore treat missing feedback as unobserved rather than positive, check user consent and data-use permissions before ingestion, and turn recurring production failures into graded regression fixtures before feeding them into training. Together with retention caps and external anchors, these safeguards turn self-improvement into a concrete collection plan with explicit sampling budgets, automated verification stages, and human review gates. Because rollout generation scales out easily across accelerators while sandboxed verification and human review have tight throughput ceilings, the collection pipeline must pace sampling against downstream checking capacity so unverified traces never swamp staging storage.

15.8 Collection Pipeline Architecture

During a collection campaign, a fleet of rollout workers can finish hundreds of candidate traces while downstream test sandboxes are saturated. A multi-predicate check can also stall waiting on a single lagging reviewer after earlier checks have already passed. Because generation, verification, and dataset publication run at different speeds, structuring collection as an asynchronous pipeline decouples the stages within explicit capacity limits. When downstream verifiers or review queues fill up, backpressure throttles or pauses new rollouts before in-flight outputs exhaust staging storage.

15.8.1 Pipeline responsibilities

If a rollout worker tears down its execution sandbox before persisting the candidate workspace bytes, or if a network retry submits a duplicate trace without a stable run identity, the collector either loses the evidence for an accepted run or double-counts examples in the published corpus. To give every artifact and buffer reservation an explicit owner across handoffs, the pipeline divides into four responsibilities (table 12). These stages may share a single host or run as distributed services across a cluster; the division fixes ownership of work, state, and evidence rather than hardware layout.

At each handoff, the pipeline checks an explicit contract. In a software repair collection run, for instance, the handoff from capture to assessment binds the persisted workspace bytes (ws_r20) to the acceptance policy (repair_accept_v1) and records which execution artifacts are available for the declared learning use within the trace capture limits. The verification cascade then runs the predicates of section 15.5, separating whether a rollout solved the task from whether it qualifies as a demonstration, a preference pair, or a rollout fixture. If a predicate cannot be completed immediately, the pipeline records its state as pending or unknown rather than treating it as a failure. Slow stages such as human review or formal verification pull cases from a dedicated, bounded queue; transferring a record to that queue requires an acknowledgment that buffer space is reserved, so stalled reviews cannot pile up and exhaust staging storage.

↩︎ Trace capture limits and retention boundaries are established in Trace Retention and Privacy.

Because distributed workers and network links fail, message delivery is retried. Every submission is therefore bound to its collection run identifier, artifact version, and assessment policy. When a retry arrives with an identical payload under the same identifier, the stage returns the existing decision. If the payload differs from the earlier submission under that identifier, the pipeline routes the conflict to an operator queue. A retry never publishes duplicate training examples or frees a staging reservation twice, which sets up the quantitative sizing of those staging reservations.

↩︎ Semantic deduplication of retried writes, which acknowledges an identical retry and rejects a conflicting payload under the same identity, is established in The storage engine contract.

15.8.2 Collection credits and staging reserves

Sizing those staging reservations around average verifier throughput fails as soon as a defective test fixture or unresponsive dependency forces every verifier slot to run until its hard timeout. In steady state, when completed rollouts arrive at verification at an average rate \(\ArrivalRate\) and \(\WorkerCount\) equivalent verifier slots each require a mean service time \(\MeanVerifierServiceTime\) per record, the pool provides an aggregate service capacity of \(\WorkerCount/\MeanVerifierServiceTime\) records per second at nominal utilization \(\ServingUtilization=\ArrivalRate \MeanVerifierServiceTime/\WorkerCount\). Little’s law (theorem 5.1) gives the mean number of records in service as \(\ArrivalRate \MeanVerifierServiceTime = \ServingUtilization \WorkerCount\), so the nominal utilization is also the fraction of slots occupied on average.

↩︎ Little’s law, which ties mean occupancy to arrival rate and residence time, is established in Residence time and service rate.

That steady-state baseline breaks when a stall forces every verifier execution to block until hitting its hard timeout \(\VerifierTimeout\), collapsing aggregate checking capacity from \(\WorkerCount/\MeanVerifierServiceTime\) to \(\WorkerCount/\VerifierTimeout\). If rollouts continue arriving at rate \(\ArrivalRate\), unassessed records accumulate in staging at a net rate of \(\ArrivalRate - \WorkerCount/\VerifierTimeout\) records per second. If each record reserves buffer space up to a maximum per-record ceiling \(\RecordSizeCeiling\), a staging storage pool of total capacity \(\StagingStoreCapacity\) that is empty when the burst begins is exhausted after

\[\StagingExhaustionTime=\frac{\StagingStoreCapacity}{\left(\ArrivalRate-\WorkerCount/\VerifierTimeout\right)\RecordSizeCeiling}.\]

A store that already holds records at onset is exhausted sooner, in proportion to the space they occupy. Crossing an alert threshold does not halt staging growth immediately, because two sequential waves of records still arrive before the buffer stabilizes. First, while the controller takes \(\ControllerReactionDelay\) to detect the stall and close dispatch, the dispatcher continues launching replacement rollouts, so staging keeps accumulating net reservations at rate \((\ArrivalRate-\WorkerCount/\VerifierTimeout)\RecordSizeCeiling\) throughout that delay. Second, at the moment dispatch finally closes, the rollouts still executing in the fleet hold up to \(\InflightRolloutReservation\) in reservations that can all complete before stalled verifiers drain them. Subtracting both waves from total capacity \(\StagingStoreCapacity\) bounds the high watermark (the fraction of the store at which the alert must trigger):

\[\text{watermark} \le 1-\frac{\InflightRolloutReservation + \ControllerReactionDelay\left(\ArrivalRate-\WorkerCount/\VerifierTimeout\right)\RecordSizeCeiling}{\StagingStoreCapacity}.\]

Notebook 15.4 evaluates how fast staging fills on a representative verification fleet and where dispatch must stop.

Napkin Math 15.4: How long a staging store survives a timeout burst
Problem: A hypothetical collector runs 1,024 concurrent rollouts that each take 45 s. 512 verifier slots take 16.875 s per record on average. Each record reserves a ceiling of 8 MB in a 32 GB staging store. Treat completions as a steady flow and all slots as interchangeable. A defective fixture then makes every test run to its 120 s timeout. How long until the store is fully reserved, and where must dispatch stop if the controller needs 30 s to act?

Math: Rollouts complete at 22.8 records per second against a nominal capacity of 30.3, giving a utilization of 0.75; by Little’s law, that is also the average fraction of the 512 slots in use. During the burst, capacity falls to 4.3 records per second, so staging grows by 18.5 records, or 147.9 MB/s of reservations, every second. Starting from an empty store, it is fully reserved after 216.3 s, about 3.6 min. When dispatch stops, the rollouts already running still hold up to 8.2 GB of reservations, and growth over the 30 s reaction delay adds 4.4 GB. Subtracting both from the 32 GB store places the high watermark at or below 60 percent of the store.

Systems insight: Size staging buffers for service-time collapses rather than nominal steady-state utilization. Even at a modest nominal utilization of 0.75, a verifier stall fills available storage in only 3.6 min. The exhaustion time \(\StagingExhaustionTime\) shrinks proportionally as the record ceiling \(\RecordSizeCeiling\) increases and scales linearly with total store size \(\StagingStoreCapacity\). Watermarks must account for work already in flight and controller reaction latency, backed by hard credit caps when feedback loops lag.

The in-flight term in that headroom shows why admission control must count unfinished work rather than queue depth. Queue length reflects past arrivals, while rollouts admitted before a verifier stall complete and emit records long after the dispatcher stops issuing new work. Admission controllers therefore limit both the count of active records and their aggregate reserved bytes, and they charge each reservation at the per-record ceiling \(\RecordSizeCeiling\) rather than the mean size, because mean sizes set steady-state storage while the ceiling bounds a worst-case burst.

A collection credit11 enforces both constraints through a single accounting token: every admitted rollout acquires one credit from a shared pool, reserving storage for its maximum possible payload throughout rollout execution, intermediate staging, and downstream assessment. The credit returns to the pool only when the record reaches a durable terminal disposition or completes an acknowledged handoff to another capacity-controlled queue. Consequently, every unit of pending work is explicitly accounted for across the entire cluster. When a trajectory exceeds its expected allocation, the capture harness enforces an explicit truncation policy rather than silently clipping required state. For example, it records the partial execution trace with marked omissions and flags the artifact as ineligible for training regimes that require full fidelity. Because one credit spans all four responsibilities, the pool also bounds work that has left the collector but not yet reached a disposition (figure 4).

11 Credit-Based Flow Control: The mechanism originates in network switches, where a transmitter may forward a cell only while holding a credit for a free downstream buffer slot, so buffers cannot overflow however bursty the traffic (Kung et al. 1994). The receiver returns the credit only after draining the slot, the analog of returning a collection credit upon durable disposition.

Kung, Hsiang-Tsung, Trevor Blackwell, and Alan Chapman. 1994. “Credit-Based Flow Control for ATM Networks: Credit Update Protocol, Adaptive Credit Allocation, and Statistical Multiplexing.” Proceedings of the Conference on Communications Architectures, Protocols and Applications (SIGCOMM), 101–14. https://doi.org/10.1145/190809.190324.

↪ Credit windows between cooperating agents, and the throughput a window of fixed size allows, are developed in Credit-based flow control.

The credit pool directly bounds the count and aggregate record volume of all outstanding runs. This mechanism governs staging buffers, not auxiliary execution resources. Accelerator memory for model inference, sandbox container filesystems, verifier scratch disks, and external object stores each require separate capacity pools with independent accounting. Storing a lightweight URI reference in a message queue does not bound the multi-gigabyte container image or execution trace to which it points. Robust admission controllers verify reservations across all requisite resource pools up front. This pre-allocation invariant ensures that a pipeline stage never acquires one scarce resource, such as an accelerator slot, only to block indefinitely waiting for an unreserved downstream buffer. That credit bound holds even when a completion burst arrives while verification is paused, as notebook 15.5 works out for a hypothetical collector.

Napkin Math 15.5: Reserve capacity before the completion burst
Problem: A hypothetical collector provisions 12 end-to-end credits, each reserving a per-record ceiling of about 2.10 MB. Currently, 4 records sit staged in storage and 8 rollouts are actively executing, each holding an allocated credit while verification is temporarily paused. May the dispatcher admit an additional rollout? Furthermore, once all active rollouts finish and verification resumes, how long will this accumulated cohort take to drain?

Variables: 2 verifiers each assess one record at a time in exactly 10 s, including durable disposition; no new rollout is admitted during the drain, and transfer and publication add no delay.

Math: The 12 outstanding runs hold every credit: staged records reserve 8.39 MB and running rollouts 16.78 MB, for 25.17 MB (exactly 25165824 bytes) with 0 credits free, so the dispatcher admits nothing. Draining the queue takes ⌈12 / 2⌉ = 6 waves of 10 s, or 60 s.

Systems insight: Total byte consumption is strictly bounded by credits multiplied by the ceiling (\(\RecordSizeCeiling\)), independent of arrival timing, while drain latency scales as ⌈credits / verifiers⌉ times service time. Adding verifiers accelerates queue drainage without increasing the byte bound; expanding credits raises both. Engineers should dimension credit pools against physical staging limits and scale verifiers to meet drain deadlines, treating a slower assessment as a longer drain with the reservation still actively held.

The two models bound different quantities. Arrival and service rates govern how rapidly an outage consumes available headroom, while credit limits cap total buffer consumption regardless of timing. Production telemetry must continuously track queue depths, allocated vs. physically consumed bytes, the age of the oldest pending assessment, verification pass rates, and downstream sink throughput. Retries, escalated evaluations, and diagnostic reviews must all consume allocations from this same credit pool. When external dependencies fail, failure containment mechanisms prevent repeated re-verification attempts from amplifying an isolated service outage into cluster-wide failure, leaving steady-state capacity planning to determine which stage limits throughput when the pipeline is healthy.

↩︎ Circuit breakers, coordinated retries, and bulkheads that keep retry amplification from spreading a dependency outage are established in Tool Circuit Breakers.

15.8.3 The collection bottleneck

Under-provisioning cheap CPU verifier slots can throttle an entire accelerator fleet, leaving expensive generation hardware sitting idle while trajectories wait to be checked. While credit controls prevent staging buffers from overflowing during an outage, steady-state capacity planning compares generation, verification, and storage to find where throughput actually binds. With \(\AcceleratorCount\) accelerators each spending \(\AttemptGenerationTime\) accelerator-seconds per attempt, generation has a service ceiling of \(\AcceleratorCount / \AttemptGenerationTime\) attempts per second. With \(\WorkerCount\) verifier slots and expected slot service time \(\ExpectedCheckingTime\) per attempt from equation 1, assessment has a service ceiling of \(\WorkerCount / \ExpectedCheckingTime\). Charging every write at its per-record ceiling \(\RecordSizeCeiling\) gives a conservative provisioned storage rate of \(\DurableStoreWriteBandwidth / \RecordSizeCeiling\) for sustained write bandwidth \(\DurableStoreWriteBandwidth\). With enough credits, work, and scheduling capacity to saturate the stages, the idealized planning rate \(\SustainableAttemptRate\) is:

\[\SustainableAttemptRate=\min\left(\frac{\AcceleratorCount}{\AttemptGenerationTime},\ \frac{\WorkerCount}{\ExpectedCheckingTime},\ \frac{\DurableStoreWriteBandwidth}{\RecordSizeCeiling}\right). \tag{4}\]

Equation 4 identifies the binding stage in this stipulated model. Its planned admitted-record rate is \(\SustainableAttemptRate \cdot \AdmissionRouteYield\), where \(\AdmissionRouteYield\) is the route yield. Actual throughput can be lower because of credits, scheduling, or another shared service. Smaller writes can exceed the conservative storage term, so that term alone does not diagnose an observed storage bottleneck.

The bound separates the stage that limits throughput from the stage that drives cost. In the illustrated low-priced deterministic route, verifiers occupy CPU slots that cost pennies per hour compared with accelerator instances, yet starving that pool stalls the whole collector. Under the notebook’s active-resource accounting, unused capacity is redeployed or unbilled, and generation compute and task yield dominate the charge per admitted record. A dedicated fleet billed during idle wall time has a different cost boundary. Deterministic verification is not inexpensive for every workload. A model judge or a human reviewer can change that split, and Exercise 5 in section 15.14 prices a route in which human review dominates. Notebook 15.6 evaluates this economic trade-off on a representative deployment.

Napkin Math 15.6: The cheapest stage can set the rate
Problem: A hypothetical collector uses 64 accelerators, each spending 60 s of accelerator time per attempt at $2.50 per hour. It checks attempts with the admission route of notebook 15.2, whose cheaper order occupies 6.24 s of slot time per attempt and admits 20.52 percent of attempts, on 4 verifier slots priced at $0.04 per slot-hour. Records of at most 8 MB go to a store that sustains 200 MB/s. Assume sufficient end-to-end credits and no further shared service bottleneck, charge each write at its record ceiling, and bill only active accelerator and occupied verifier time, with unused capacity redeployed or unbilled. Which stage binds this planning model, and what is the active-resource charge per admitted record?

Math: By equation 4, generation supports 64 / 60 s = 1.07 attempts per second, assessment 4 / 6.24 s = 0.64, and conservative provisioned storage supports 200 MB/s / 8 MB = 25. Assessment binds in this model, and matching generation needs 1.07 × 6.24 s, rounded up to 7 slots. Generation costs $0.042 per attempt; adding checking and dividing by the yield gives $0.20 per admitted record, of which checking is 0.2 percent.

Systems insight: Throughput bottlenecks and cost drivers are often decoupled. In this scenario, running only 4 inexpensive verifier slots leaves 39.9 percent of generation capacity unused. Despite this bottleneck, the financial cost of an admitted record is dominated by generation expense divided by yield, following the cost per accepted task formulation (equation 1.2). Adding verifier slots raises the idealized rate until generation saturates while leaving the active-resource charge per record unchanged. With a dedicated fleet billed during idle wall time, greater utilization can instead lower the bill per emitted record. Holding active work per attempt and resource rates fixed, halving the yield to 10.26 percent doubles the unrounded cost per admitted record, displayed to the nearest cent as $0.41. Fixture design and sampling policy control that yield; their measured cost and coverage consequences determine whether a change is worthwhile.

15.8.4 Artifact capture and resource release

Tearing down a rollout container before its logs and workspace diffs reach durable storage destroys the only copy of the attempt, while reassigning a pooled container before background writers exit lets a stale rollout pollute the next task. Collection pipelines therefore inherit the runtime’s sandbox lifecycle and reset contracts. Rollout workers must persist all candidate artifacts, interaction traces, and evaluation evidence to durable storage before tearing down the execution sandbox. A pooled environment cannot be reassigned until background writer processes terminate, stale network credentials and access tokens are revoked, and filesystem reset postconditions pass. If a downstream verification stage needs interactive access to unpersisted sandbox state, it must hold that environment under an explicit time-bounded lease and operational budget; otherwise, the checker logs the assessment as unavailable and releases the host.

When a worker’s dispatch lease expires, expiration does not instantly terminate the executing process or roll back its external side effects. A replacement worker must therefore establish explicit ownership and verify fresh admission checks, while late-arriving outputs from the orphaned worker remain strictly quarantined to their original run. Similarly, if container cleanup fails or an external side effect leaves an indeterminate state, the owning process retains responsibility under the trajectory saga, even if the associated trajectory is discarded from training. Releasing a collection credit frees buffer staging, but it does not absolve the runtime of its teardown and cleanup obligations. Once physical movement from execution to durable disposition is bounded and accounted for, data governance determines artifact admissibility. It asks which upstream source versions justify inclusion, what lossy or privacy-preserving transformations were applied, and which dataset split partitions and license restrictions govern the resulting weights.

↩︎ The trajectory saga, which keeps a compensation obligation open while an external effect remains unresolved, is established in The Trajectory Saga Pattern.

15.9 Trajectory Provenance Tracking

When an automated scrubbing pass strips sensitive network addresses from raw collection record trace_r19_01 (from collection run collection_run_r19_01 and candidate workspace ws_r20) to create trace_r19_01_redacted_v1, the transformed artifact cannot silently overwrite the source record and inherit its admission labels. Months after an execution trace enters a training corpus, curators must still be able to answer which collection run produced the sequence, which validation criteria were checked against which environment fixtures, whether the organization retains legal permission to train on the incorporated source code or API payloads, and whether sanitization invalidated the runtime state that justified the agent’s next action. A preserved execution trace and its evaluation scores cannot answer these questions on their own. In a production curation pipeline, provenance provides the immutable, versioned record that answers them across every derivation of a trace, giving trace_r19_01_redacted_v1 an independent identity and its own admission decision.

15.9.1 The lineage envelope

When a source repository revokes training permission or a grader bug invalidates a week of checks, an unindexed store of raw JSON traces cannot identify which corpus members must be pulled. A lineage envelope12 prevents that blind spot by recording the origin, dependencies, transformations, and admission decisions of each individual record version. Table 13 outlines its six dimensions. Rather than embedding inline copies of sensitive or high-volume execution state, envelope fields point to content-addressed, access-controlled artifacts.

12 Why- and Where-Provenance: Database research distinguished two questions about a derived datum: which source records justify its existence (why-provenance) and which source locations its values were copied from (where-provenance) (Buneman et al. 2001). A trajectory record needs both. Where-provenance traces each retained field to the run, event, and transformation that produced it, and why-provenance traces its admission to the assessments that justified it, so a withdrawn source or a revised criterion can be followed to every affected corpus member.

Buneman, Peter, Sanjeev Khanna, and Wang-Chiew Tan. 2001. “Why and Where: A Characterization of Data Provenance.” Database Theory – ICDT 2001, Lecture notes in computer science, vol. 1973: 316–30. https://doi.org/10.1007/3-540-44503-X_20.

The envelope must record only what the runtime interfaces actually reveal. When an agent executes against locally controlled model weights, the envelope binds the loaded base model and adapter checkpoint digests. Hosted inference endpoints, however, typically expose only a reported version string and request configuration; in those cases, the envelope documents those exact parameters rather than fabricating an unverifiable weight hash. Similarly, a prompt-template identifier fails to capture dynamic context injected at runtime, and a container base-image digest omits mutable mounted volumes or external network state. Systems engineers must document omissions explicitly rather than relying on identifiers of ambiguous scope.

Evaluation assessments bind directly to the exact byte representation tested. If a trajectory earns a passing score on a targeted capability test, that verdict applies only to that evaluated representation; any pending criterion remains pending when the record enters cold storage. The downstream evaluation cascade alone determines whether the available evidence authorizes a specific learning use, and cryptographic integrity checks then verify that the assessed byte representation is the exact one a downstream trainer reads.

15.9.2 Integrity and authentication

A trainer decides what to train on by reading the corpus manifest, so it must be able to confirm two things before the first gradient. The first is that the manifest is the one curation issued. The second is that no record was altered or swapped after admission, whether by a broken pipeline script or by anyone with write access to the store. Integrity mechanisms answer those two questions. They establish unmodified bytes, not correct content or permitted use. A cryptographic digest identifies a specified byte representation under standard collision-resistance assumptions. For that digest to remain meaningful across system components, the lineage envelope must fix both the canonical serialization scheme and the schema version over which the hash was calculated. Comparing an artifact against an expected digest detects bit corruption and unauthorized edits. Storing an unauthenticated digest directly beside mutable content in an object store, however, provides no protection, because an adversary or a broken pipeline script can overwrite the data and the digest together.

To guarantee authenticity, an authenticated manifest cryptographically binds expected artifact digests and associated metadata to a trusted issuing authority. Systems commonly use a message authentication code (MAC), such as HMAC as specified in RFC 2104 (Krawczyk et al. 1997), computed with a secret key held outside the dataset in a key management service. Because any party holding a shared symmetric key can forge a valid MAC, production architectures often prefer asymmetric digital signatures (such as Ed25519) when datasets are distributed across multiple worker nodes. The manifest must record the cryptographic algorithm, key version, and authorized issuing scope, and key rotation policies must match the operational lifetime of the corpus. Verification confirms only that the authenticated byte representation has not changed since issuance; it covers nothing outside that boundary.

Krawczyk, Hugo, Mihir Bellare, and Ran Canetti. 1997. HMAC: Keyed-Hashing for Message Authentication. RFC 2104. Internet Engineering Task Force. https://doi.org/10.17487/RFC2104.

13 Merkle Tree: A tree in which each internal node holds the hash of its children, introduced to build digital signatures from a conventional one-way function (Merkle 1988). Its useful property is that proving one leaf belongs under a trusted root needs only the sibling hashes along one path, logarithmic in the number of leaves. A corpus manifest can therefore commit to millions of record digests under one root and still verify a single member cheaply, but the root commits only to the leaves that were hashed.

Merkle, Ralph C. 1988. “A Digital Signature Based on a Conventional Encryption Function.” Advances in Cryptology — CRYPTO ’87, Lecture notes in computer science, vol. 293: 369–78. https://doi.org/10.1007/3-540-48184-2_32.

Cryptographic integrity must not be confused with semantic correctness. A faithfully authenticated trajectory trace can still hold hallucinated model outputs, an erroneous grader verdict, or private data that downstream consumers are prohibited from seeing. Similarly, computing a Merkle root13 over captured execution events proves that a given event belongs to the recorded tree, but it proves nothing about execution events that the logging harness dropped or failed to observe. Claims about trajectory reproducibility must therefore stay within the reconstruction and re-execution boundaries established in Recorded Trajectory Replay.

15.9.3 Derived records

Because an authenticated source record is immutable, scrubbing a private file path or API token from a recorded trace in place would break its digest and erase the only copy of what actually ran, often severing the causal link that justified the agent’s next command. At the same time, the data minimization and disclosure boundaries established in Disclosure minimization must be enforced before trajectory content enters an ingestion queue, a model-assisted curation worker, or a training store. To resolve that tension, a derived record is always created as a new version rather than an in-place modification. The derived record binds a reference to its parent trace, the versioned redaction policy and implementation, the explicit set of altered or excised fields, and any intentionally withheld content. Lineage references do not grant read authorization to restricted raw data, and systems must store original raw bytes only where retention policies explicitly permit them. When source records reach their retention expiration limit and are purged, the lineage envelope documents that boundary directly; retaining a cryptographic hash is not the same as retaining the source data. The source also keeps a reverse reference to each derived version, so that a later correction or withdrawal at the source reaches every artifact derived from it (figure 5).

Heuristic pattern matching and named-entity recognition models act as screening mechanisms rather than complete sanitization guarantees. Engineers must validate these detectors across the full output pipeline using realistic end-to-end test cases, including deeply nested payloads and generated metadata. Synthetic canaries provide an empirical measure of detector recall. The curation audit log must record how many canaries were injected, how many escaped detection, which semantic classes and text transformations were tested, and where the inspection boundary was applied. Observing zero canary escapes bounds the leak probability only for the evaluated canary distribution. False positives carry their own cost, because over-aggressive scrubbing strips necessary context and distorts the training signal.

↩︎ Canary values, planted synthetic secrets whose escape reveals a leaking path, are established in Detectors and canary values.

When scrubbing identifiers across multi-step interactions, curators use consistent pseudonyms to preserve relational dependencies across turns while preventing identity linkage beyond the access scope that the trace’s disclosure boundary permits. Tokenization effects require careful handling: replacing an identifier with a fixed-width placeholder often alters the sequence’s token count and attention boundaries because a placeholder string rarely splits into the same number of subword tokens as the original identifier, shifting every subsequent token index and misaligning any token-level loss masks that were indexed against the unredacted transcript. Similarly, reserializing a structured payload can preserve outer JSON or YAML formatting while silently breaking nested shell escaping, regular expression matches, or tool invocation syntax. Consequently, checking that an artifact parses successfully is distinct from verifying that it still supports the intended learning target.

↪ Re-verifying selected response spans against token offsets after templating or redaction shifts token boundaries is developed in Rendering with versioned templates.

Revalidation must systematically test every property that the transformation could have influenced. This includes validating record structure, ensuring action-observation consistency, verifying that the sanitized context still justifies the target action, and re-evaluating any criteria whose supporting evidence was altered. If a transformation modifies executable code or environment-dependent identifiers, the pipeline must execute the code against an isolated, authorized test fixture to obtain fresh runtime evidence, while archiving prior execution logs as historical artifacts.

Example 15.1: A transformed record needs a new decision
Scenario: During a collection run, an agent’s observation records a private system path that a subsequent diagnostic command references, and the source trace captures the successful output of that command. The curation pipeline replaces the path with a sanitized placeholder throughout trace_r19_01_redacted_v1.

Diagnosis: The recorded execution output remains valid evidence only for the original run bound to trace_r19_01; the agent never executed the sanitized placeholder path. The curator must determine whether the sanitized observation and the subsequent command still constitute a coherent learning example. A consistent placeholder can teach an agent how to parse and inspect arbitrary paths without exposing production directory structures. However, replaying the trajectory against an active execution harness requires an authorized, compatible mock fixture; otherwise, the substituted path fails to resolve. If the substitution obscures the causal distinction that prompted the agent’s action, the pipeline must reject the target for supervised training or withhold the record. The derived record inherits the source’s task grouping and train/test split assignment, while all pending evaluation verdicts remain pending until formal revalidation completes.

Systems lesson: Cryptographic integrity establishes which source artifact was transformed, whereas revalidation establishes what the transformed trajectory teaches. Neither operation can alter what physically occurred during the original execution run.

15.9.4 Use permission and withdrawal

Permission to execute an agent against a task fixture or inspect an internal repository does not convey permission to retain its artifacts, fine-tune models on its data, or publish the resulting corpus. The lineage envelope must record the explicit licensing constraints, the evidentiary basis for data retention, and the designated policy authority or human reviewer who approved data usage. This accounting must cover both the primary task assets and all third-party software dependencies pulled into the environment during execution. Automated license detectors and embedding-based similarity screens help identify suspect components, but they serve as screening tools rather than autonomous legal adjudicators.

When provenance evidence is ambiguous or incomplete, the curation pipeline must withhold the record from downstream consumers while logging the specific withholding rationale. This quarantine is use-specific: an asset held from public pretraining might remain eligible for internal safety evaluations. Furthermore, use-permission holds operate independently of task failure labels, confidentiality tiers, and fault-containment quarantine. Resolving a safety fault or task defect does not automatically grant data-usage clearance.

⇄ Contrast: Incident containment, which holds the work a suspect trajectory might touch rather than withholding a record from one use, is established in Incident Containment.

Approved data permissions operate under strict temporal and organizational scopes. To enforce policy changes, reverse references must connect each source record to its derived views, tokenized dataset partitions, and published corpus manifests. When an upstream data provider revokes permission, or when an organization updates its privacy policies, the system must propagate the restriction or withdrawal to every downstream artifact. The ingestion pipeline immediately halts new admissions under the revoked policy, while the catalog traces all distributed artifacts. Deleting a record from an active storage bucket leaves distributed offline copies and completed model parameter updates intact; managing those downstream exposures requires explicit operational ownership, retraining schedules, and audited unlearning procedures.

Checkpoint 15.2: Defend a derived record's use

Each bullet asks for a decision a curator makes before a derived record ships.

A well-architected trajectory record has a verifiable origin, an explicit transformation history, a legally scoped permission envelope, and preserved evaluation boundaries. Connecting an execution trace through this provenance graph into a corpus manifest gives membership a precise meaning: verified fitness for a declared learning consumer. Carrying one record through every stage shows how these decisions fit together.

15.10 Case Study: Curating the Repair Record

The recovery run collection_run_r19_01, whose targets section 15.6.2 selected, exercises each stage of that admission and provenance chain. Its refused edit, schema inspection, and corrected edit produced ws_r20, which satisfied repair_accept_v1. The raw trace trace_r19_01 records that sequence without altering any earlier record, neither the pending trial verdict on ws_r19 nor the unresolved earlier test operation test_r18. The curation question is which parts of this record may enter a learning artifact, for which consumer, and on what evidence.

The training objective is recovery from harness refusals, the residual behavior that the campaign’s probes left with the model. The target policy recovery_targets_v1 already fixed which tokens contribute direct supervised loss: the schema query e3, the corrected edit e4, and the final summary e6. The refused proposal e2, the test summary e5, the task prompt, and the intermediate tool responses stay as conditioning context. Target selection is only the first stage of admission. The record that actually enters a corpus is a transformed view of trace_r19_01, so the remaining stages must verify that view, bind its provenance, and publish it to a manifest.

15.10.1 Admission of the derived view

Replacing sensitive fixture paths in trace_r19_01 with synthetic placeholders produces trace_r19_01_redacted_v1, but if that redaction strips the file names the agent needed to deduce the schema, training on the redacted trace teaches the model to guess rather than inspect. While the underlying execution stems directly from the original run, admission filtering must verify whether this sanitized view preserves the causal chain of the recovery lesson rather than assuming the raw run’s pass carries over. The admission pipeline does not demand byte-for-byte fidelity with historical environment state; it requires that the transformed trace still support supervised imitation of the selected targets. Table 14 evaluates this derived view against the curation cascade’s admission predicates, where each passing verdict in a production manifest links directly to an underlying verification log.

Because each predicate evaluates to true, the pipeline admits the transformed example as a verified decision demonstration. Had path substitution stripped the file names essential for the agent to deduce the correct schema, target suitability (\(\text{chk}_{\text{target,demo}}\)) would have failed. Furthermore, while this redacted trace is valid for token-level imitation learning, it cannot serve a downstream consumer that requires executable replay, because executing the synthetic paths would require spinning up a fresh environment with matching mock fixtures. A single execution lineage can therefore support fine-tuning while being disqualified from offline replay benchmarks. Neither a valid cryptographic digest nor an overall task pass provides blanket approval across different training regimens.

15.10.2 Corpus manifest publication

Admitting trace_r19_01_redacted_v1 into a storage bucket without binding its target mask, split group, and grader versions leaves a downstream trainer free to supervise the refused edit e2 by mistake or leak the task into evaluation. Publishing the curated trajectory to the corpus release repair_demonstrations_v1 prevents that drift by recording every link in the artifact’s provenance chain inside the corpus manifest. For this member, the manifest binds the record to its originating run (collection_run_r19_01), task identifier, fixture snapshot, candidate code patch, and evaluation grader versions. It references both the transformed trace (trace_r19_01_redacted_v1) and the target selection policy (recovery_targets_v1), while logging the specific data governance approvals, task group assignments, and split partition versions. Any missing metadata or inactive model dependencies are explicitly tagged as unavailable rather than omitted.

At the corpus level, the manifest catalogs the source data pools, sampling weights, token accounting budgets, domain coverage metrics, and the formal evaluation protocol. Maintaining clear distinctions among raw interaction counts, stored trace tokens, and selected supervised target tokens prevents deceptive accounting. During training, serialization formats and loss masks enforce these target boundaries, ensuring that future trajectory steps never leak into earlier conditioning windows.

This curated trajectory yields a single demonstration artifact. It cannot be repurposed as a preference pair, because the rejected proposal e2 and the later edit e4 follow different prefixes (e4 comes after the refusal and the schema lookup), so they are not alternative continuations of one context. A preference artifact would need competing completions sampled from one saved branch point. Similarly, publishing an environment for reinforcement learning rollouts requires exporting the task fixture, harness configurations, and dynamic graders, rather than static token sequences.

Publishing an immutable corpus manifest establishes a versioned training baseline, which remains separate from deployment governance for any resulting model. Tracking lineage guarantees that if an upstream fixture defect or data privacy request emerges later, pipeline operators can cleanly trace and purge all derived artifacts across downstream model versions. At the infrastructure layer, ephemeral execution sandboxes are destroyed and billed compute charges are settled independently of whether a trace reaches the corpus manifest.

15.10.3 Cost per emitted artifact

Because every initiated run incurs compute and verification charges whether or not it reaches the manifest, raw trial volume is a deceptive metric for collection economics. A pipeline may execute thousands of autonomous interactions yet emit only a small handful of usable training samples, as trajectories fail safety filters, duplicate existing task solutions, crash during tool dispatch, or remain stalled in asynchronous evaluation queues. Consequently, systems engineers must evaluate collection economics using the cost per emitted artifact, amortizing all spent compute and infrastructure overhead across the accepted dataset.

For a given collection cohort and accounting cutoff, let \(\CollectionCohortCost\) represent the total monetary expenditure incurred across environment provisioning, inference generation, tool sandbox execution, automated verification, human review, redaction transformations, and storage. Let \(\EmittedArtifactCount\) denote the number of valid artifacts emitted for a designated consumer by that cutoff. The cost per emitted artifact is defined as

\[\CostPerEmittedArtifact=\frac{\CollectionCohortCost}{\EmittedArtifactCount},\qquad \EmittedArtifactCount>0. \tag{5}\]
