The numerator \(\CollectionCohortCost\) captures all unproductive runs, rejected proposals, and pipeline failures; if a cohort yields zero usable artifacts (\(\EmittedArtifactCount = 0\)), the pipeline registers the incurred financial loss without dividing by zero. Structurally, equation 5 mirrors the cost per accepted task formulation (equation 1.2): the unit cost equals the average cost per attempt divided by the end-to-end emission yield. This metric isolates data acquisition efficiency, distinguishing curation costs from both runtime inference serving and subsequent model fine-tuning.

Napkin Math 15.7: Cost follows the emitted artifact
Problem: A collection cohort initiates 500 collection attempts, incurring $300 in fully accounted costs across environment provisioning, generation, tool calls, automated verification, curation transforms, storage, and human review. The demonstration selector emits at most one artifact per task. What does each emitted artifact cost, and which operational lever most effectively reduces that price?

Math: The selected-and-emitted row of table 15 yields an emission rate of 15 / 500 = 3 percent. The average cost per attempt is $300 / 500 = $0.60. Applying equation 5 yields $0.60 / 3 percent = $20 per emitted artifact. This emission yield differs from a task-completion rate, because the curator caps repeated solutions for the same task and several nonselected runs successfully resolved their repair targets. It is also far below the route yield of notebook 15.2, which counts the records that pass one route’s predicates. Emission yield charges every initiated attempt with the per-task cap, redundancy decisions, ineligibility, and whatever is still pending at the cutoff.

Systems insight: Because unit cost equals attempt cost divided by emission yield, curation yield serves as the primary economic lever. For example, raising yield to 6 percent cuts the unit price in half to $10. With 97 percent of attempts failing to emit a usable artifact, engineering effort should focus on diagnosing nonselection causes before purchasing additional generation compute. High redundancy indicates that the system should launch fewer attempts per task; ineligibility points to split or permission defects caught too late; and backlogged pending evaluations point to verification bottlenecks.

A published manifest documents what was emitted, verifies eligibility across all admission gates, isolates supervision targets, and tallies total acquisition cost. Operational efficiency and curation hygiene, however, are prerequisites rather than guarantees of model improvement. The test of a curated corpus is offline evaluation against held-out task groups under controlled benchmark protocols, where neither a low cost per artifact nor a high emission yield can substitute for task performance.

15.11 Fallacies and Pitfalls

A data collection pipeline can preserve every execution record faithfully and still assemble a counterproductive training set. The mistakes below stem from confusing raw trace telemetry with the deliberate pedagogical choices required to train an agent, and each carries tangible computational or behavioral costs.

Fallacy: A successful task makes every action in its trace a suitable demonstration.

Task acceptance evaluates end-to-end task completion, whereas target selection decides which specific token sequences should receive gradient supervision. Consider an agent that successfully debugs a codebase after several stumbling attempts. Its transcript might include an initial malformed JSON tool call, a parser rejection from the runtime environment, and a subsequent correction. While that malformed call must remain in the transcript as conditioning context so the agent understands the environment’s feedback, it must never be included in the training loss as a target. In the recovery worksheet, a typical recovery trajectory contains 18,500 stored tokens, but only 2,000 of those tokens are designated as supervised targets (section 15.6). Supervising the entire accepted trace forces the model to memorize and reproduce its own intermediate errors. In a sound curation pipeline, record validity (whether the telemetry accurately reflects what happened), task acceptance (whether the agent fulfilled its objective), and target eligibility (whether an action should be reinforced) are three distinct filtering decisions.

Pitfall: Fine-tuning on every failure before probing its cause.

When an agent fails, the easiest operational reaction is often to bundle the failed trajectories into a fine-tuning dataset. In the intervention ladder worksheet, systematic counterfactual probes demonstrate that 90 percent of observed failures originate in the supporting infrastructure below the model weights, such as malformed tool schemas, missing environment variables, or overly aggressive sandbox timeouts (section 15.2.2). Retraining the model across the entire failure corpus requires only $58 of accelerator time, and that modest price creates a dangerous illusion of progress. Fine-tuning merely encourages the model to memorize ad hoc compensations for broken tooling. As soon as an external API changes or the brittle workaround misfires, the failure resurfaces. Systems engineers should run counterfactual probes first, fix defects in tools and runtime environments directly, and restrict fine-tuning to the remaining 300 genuine behavioral failures.

Fallacy: A random split of collected traces holds out unseen tasks.

Applying uniform random sampling across collected trajectories partitions individual attempts rather than underlying problem instances. When an evaluation harness collects 8 attempts per task and allocates a 80 percent fraction to training, 83.2 percent of the tasks end up present in both the training and test sets (section 15.4). Under this configuration, test metrics reflect the agent’s ability to recall previously seen problem setups rather than its capability to generalize to novel tasks. Data splitting must occur at the granularity of task groups, with environment-seed variants inheriting their group’s assignment. Furthermore, any downstream derivatives (redacted transcripts, paraphrased prompts, or deduplicated variants) must inherit the assignment of their source task so that syntactic transformations do not accidentally leak problem state across the evaluation boundary.

Pitfall: Cheapening the verifier cascade by dropping or loosening a predicate.

When verification overhead begins to strain cluster budgets, engineers often attempt to reduce costs by removing expensive check predicates or loosening test assertions. This strategy alters the semantic definition of success rather than optimizing the system. In the cascade worksheet, reordering evaluation stages based on their computational cost per rejected candidate saved 16.9 percent of total verification work while maintaining the exact same candidate yield of 20.52 percent (section 15.5.3). Dropping a verification check or silently discarding unresolved edge cases shifts the distribution of accepted trajectories, degrading the downstream training corpus. Performance optimizations should focus on scheduling predicates efficiently under fixed acceptance criteria. In addition, execution pipelines must isolate and report inconclusive runs separately rather than collapsing them into either rejections or unqualified admissions.

Fallacy: Setting a recovery percentage by record count fixes the training mixture.

Trajectory counts provide an unreliable unit of measure when balancing mixed training datasets. A curation system operates across three distinct metrics: raw trajectory records, total stored context tokens, and actively supervised target tokens. In the token budget worksheet, recovery trajectories make up 25 percent of total records, but they represent 44.97 percent of stored tokens and 39.06 percent of selected training targets (section 15.6). Because recovery sequences involve multi-step error correction and verbose environment feedback, their token footprint differs substantially from standard trajectories. Specifying mixture proportions solely by record count miscalculates both the gradient signal allocated to recovery behaviors and the computational budget required for training. Engineers must define training mixtures in terms of supervised target tokens, and validate the resulting policy resilience against held-out task benchmarks under a declared compute budget.

Pitfall: Sizing collection staging from average verifier utilization.

Dimensioning staging storage against average verification throughput invites buffer exhaustion during outages. A verifier pool running at an average utilization of 0.75 appears to have substantial headroom during normal operation. However, automated verification runtimes suffer from bimodal service times. When a faulty test fixture, infrastructure timeout, or broken container environment forces every evaluation to run until its hard execution deadline, service throughput collapses while incoming agent trajectories continue to arrive. In the burst exhaustion worksheet, this prolonged tail latency exhausts the staging store in approximately 3.6 min (section 15.8). Buffer capacity must be bounded by worst-case service latency rather than nominal arrival rates. Robust ingestion architectures use credit-based flow control to restrict outstanding verifier reservations, sizing storage watermarks to absorb both in-flight execution bursts and downstream controller response delays.

15.12 Summary

A trajectory record and a training example answer different questions. The record says what happened under stated conditions. The example asserts that some part of what happened is worth reproducing, for a named learner, and will be judged against evidence that learner never saw. Every mechanism in the chapter keeps those two apart. Probes keep system defects out of the weights. Task-group splits keep evaluation evidence out of selection. Target selection keeps a faithfully recorded mistake in context and out of the loss. Lineage keeps a transformed record from inheriting a decision made about its source.

Collection credits, lineage envelopes, and the corpus manifest make those decisions operational. The price of the result follows the book’s cost-per-accepted-task form twice over. Checking work per admitted record is cascade cost divided by yield, and cost per emitted artifact is attempt cost divided by emission yield, so yield is the lever in both. Self-improvement changes the source distribution with every round, which is why the manifest keeps demonstrations, comparisons, and rollout fixtures distinct and why external assessment stays outside the loop. The quantitative decisions of the chapter rest on the relations collected in table 16.

Key Takeaways: Give every learning artifact a declared use
  • Weights are the last remedy, not the first: Counterfactual probes place many failures in context, schemas, or the runtime, where a reversible repair removes them for every model version. Training earns only the residual that survives, and demonstrations, preference comparisons, and rollout fixtures each carry their own admission conditions.
  • A split protects a claim, not a file: With several attempts per task, a random split over traces puts most tasks on both sides, so the test score rewards recall of solved siblings. Assign whole task groups, carry the assignment into every derived record, and fix it before collection begins.
  • Cheaper checking must admit the same corpus: Ordering independent, fixed-cost predicates within permissible orders by cost per rejection, \(\CheckSlotTime_i/(1-\CheckPassProbability_i)\), cuts verification work at an unchanged accepted set. Dropping or loosening a predicate buys a different corpus, so its lower unit price is not a saving.
  • A recorded mistake is context, not a target: Recovery records keep the error and its refusal as conditioning history and select only the assessed correction as supervision. Mixtures are specified in selected targets and budgeted in stored tokens, because context per target differs by class.
  • Self-selected data narrows what it rewards: When the policy supplies its own records, rising acceptance can hide the recoveries the selector rarely keeps. Distinct action sequences per fixture, pass@1 against pass@\(\IndependentAttemptCount\), and an external anchor show whether the loop is still learning or only repeating itself.
  • Staging is sized for the burst, not the average: A verifier timeout burst, not average utilization, exhausts reserved staging, so each run holds a credit at the record ceiling until durable disposition. The cheapest stage can bind the illustrated planning rate while generation divided by yield sets its active-resource price.
  • The manifest is the contract the trainer reads: Records and derived artifacts bind to source versions, checks, transformations, permissions, split membership, and intended consumers. Integrity proves unmodified bytes, not truth or permission, so every derived record earns its own admission decision.

Verified trajectory post-training (principle 13) now has a concrete decision boundary: evidence supports a specified learning use, and the corpus manifest carries that decision through selection and transformation so the trainer can tell context from targets. The trained policy still faces evaluation and still runs under the runtime’s authority and resource controls.

15.13 Historical Perspective and Further Reading

The principles governing trajectory curation adapt lessons from data mining, information retrieval, and relational databases. Data mining first identified data leakage as a systemic failure mode where information about an evaluation target inadvertently reaches the learner through correlated records (Kaufman et al. 2012). In software modeling, duplicated source files were shown to inflate benchmark accuracy artificially until evaluation splits underwent thorough deduplication (Allamanis 2019). Modern screening pipelines borrow directly from web search, where minwise hashing estimated document resemblance from compact signatures (Broder 1997) and locality-sensitive hashing reduced high-dimensional similarity search to sublinear time (Indyk and Motwani 1998). Database query processing supplied the twin mechanisms of admission control and data provenance. Optimizers established how to order expensive filter predicates by their cost-to-selectivity rank to minimize wasted computation (Hellerstein and Stonebraker 1993), while provenance frameworks formalized which source records produced a derived value and why (Buneman et al. 2001).

Kaufman, Shachar, Saharon Rosset, Claudia Perlich, and Ori Stitelman. 2012. “Leakage in Data Mining: Formulation, Detection, and Avoidance.” ACM Transactions on Knowledge Discovery from Data (TKDD) 6 (4): 1–21.
Zelikman, Eric, Yuhuai Wu, Jesse Mu, and Noah D. Goodman. 2022. “STaR: Bootstrapping Reasoning with Reasoning.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 35: 15476–88.
Uesato, Jonathan, Nate Kushman, Ramana Kumar, Francis Song, Noah Siegel, Lisa Wang, Antonia Creswell, Geoffrey Irving, and Irina Higgins. 2022. “Solving Math Word Problems with Process- and Outcome-Based Feedback.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2211.14275, ahead of print. https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2211.14275.
Lightman, Hunter, Vineet Kosaraju, Yura Burda, Harri Edwards, Bowen Baker, Teddy Lee, Jan Leike, John Schulman, Ilya Sutskever, and Karl Cobbe. 2024. “Let’s Verify Step by Step.” The Twelfth International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).

Learning from self-generated execution traces shares an equally deep lineage in reinforcement learning and symbolic reasoning. Classical imitation learning demonstrated that a policy trained exclusively on expert demonstrations compounds its errors when deployed, because small mistakes push the agent into unfamiliar states where it cannot recover (Ross et al. 2011). Ross, Gordon, and Bagnell showed that querying an expert on the learner’s own visited states restores linear error bounds; recovery demonstrations are the systems realization of that theoretical remedy. Expert iteration formalized this dynamic into an alternating loop of generation, search-based selection, and retraining, using tree search to discover improved decisions for a neural network to imitate (Anthony et al. 2017). Bootstrapped reasoning extended this loop to language models by sampling reasoning paths and filtering for traces that reached correct answers (Zelikman et al. 2022). Process supervision then tightened verification from coarse terminal outcomes down to individual reasoning steps (Uesato et al. 2022; Lightman et al. 2024).

Every self-training method eventually meets the same problem, because the filter that selects training data is itself an imperfect part of the system it is meant to improve. Production machine learning pipelines first exposed the systemic risks of hidden feedback loops, where an operational model’s outputs silently shape its own subsequent training distribution (Sculley et al. 2015). Reward modeling demonstrated Goodhart’s law directly: excessive optimization against a learned proxy drives reward scores up while true task quality deteriorates (Gao et al. 2023). Training repeatedly on a model’s own unchecked outputs speeds model collapse, in which diversity shrinks with each round and the rare cases in the tail disappear first (Shumailov et al. 2024). Agentic execution sharpens each of these failure modes because trajectories do not exist in isolation. They execute stateful tool calls, operate under specific environment permissions, and can inadvertently manipulate or game their own evaluation harnesses. The safeguards of this chapter, from verifier cascades and disjoint task-group splits to lineage envelopes and dataset manifests (Gebru et al. 2021), apply classical systems isolation and provenance guarantees directly to the trajectory as the atomic unit of machine learning data.

Shumailov, Ilia, Zakhar Shumaylov, Yiren Zhao, Nicolas Papernot, Ross Anderson, and Yarin Gal. 2024. “AI Models Collapse When Trained on Recursively Generated Data.” Nature 631 (8022): 755–59. https://doi.org/10.1038/s41586-024-07566-y.

For further reading, start with these:

  • End-to-end arguments (Saltzer et al. 1984): The classic statement of why a function belongs where it can be implemented completely and correctly. In trajectory curation, this principle argues for repairing tool interfaces and runtime environments directly rather than training model weights to work around platform flaws.
  • Predicate migration (Hellerstein and Stonebraker 1993): The foundational database paper on ordering expensive filter checks by cost per rejection, formalizing the exchange argument used to structure multi-stage verifier cascades.
  • Imitation learning as no-regret online learning (Ross et al. 2011): The analysis demonstrating why training exclusively on clean expert prefixes leaves an agent unprepared for its own mistakes, establishing the theoretical rationale for recovery demonstrations.
  • Hidden technical debt in machine learning systems (Sculley et al. 2015): A comprehensive systems catalog of data dependencies and feedback loops that any self-improving corpus pipeline inherits.
  • Datasheets for datasets (Gebru et al. 2021): The documentation discipline that a corpus manifest adapts into versioned, machine-auditable metadata fields.
  • Reward-model overoptimization (Gao et al. 2023): A controlled empirical study showing how aggressive optimization against a learned proxy reward drives proxy scores up while true task quality degrades.
Saltzer, Jerome H., David P. Reed, and David D. Clark. 1984. “End-to-End Arguments in System Design.” ACM Transactions on Computer Systems 2 (4): 277–88. https://doi.org/10.1145/357401.357402.
Hellerstein, Joseph M., and Michael Stonebraker. 1993. “Predicate Migration: Optimizing Queries with Expensive Predicates.” Proceedings of the 1993 ACM SIGMOD International Conference on Management of Data, 267–76. https://doi.org/10.1145/170036.170078.
Sculley, D., Gary Holt, Daniel Golovin, Eugene Davydov, Todd Phillips, Dietmar Ebner, Vinay Chaudhary, Michael Young, Jean-François Crespo, and Dan Dennison. 2015. “Hidden Technical Debt in Machine Learning Systems.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 28: 2503–11.
Gao, Leo, John Schulman, and Jacob Hilton. 2023. “Scaling Laws for Reward Model Overoptimization.” Proceedings of the 40th International Conference on Machine Learning 202: 10835–66.

15.14 Exercises

Every given below is a hypothetical problem input chosen for round arithmetic, not a measurement of any system. Each problem asks for a calculation and the curation or collection decision it supports.

  1. Sizing a protected split (Basic). A collection job makes \(\IndependentAttemptCount=5\) attempts on each of 2,000 repair tasks. A colleague proposes assigning individual traces to training with probability \(\TrainingSplitFraction=0.9\) and to the final test otherwise.

    1. Compute the fraction and number of tasks whose attempts straddle the partition, and the smallest \(\IndependentAttemptCount\) at which a majority of tasks straddle.
    2. Compute the probability that a given test attempt has at least one sibling attempt of the same task in training, and state what the resulting test score measures.
    3. The team switches to a group split (section 15.4). Treating tasks as independent trials with an acceptance rate near one half, compute how many task groups the final test must hold for the standard error of its task-level acceptance rate to be at most 0.025, and what fraction of the 2,000 tasks that reserves.

  2. Ordering an admission route (Basic). A demonstration route has three independent predicates beyond record adequacy: a permitted-use lookup \(\text{chk}_{\text{use}}\) that takes 0.05 s and passes 70 percent of records, a sandboxed test run \(\text{chk}_{\text{out}}\) that takes 30 s and passes 50 percent, and a model-judged target review \(\text{chk}_{\text{target}}\) that takes 4 s and passes 80 percent. Target review does not need the test result, and the current order is \(\text{chk}_{\text{use}}\to \text{chk}_{\text{out}}\to \text{chk}_{\text{target}}\).

    1. Rank the predicates by cost per rejection (equation 2) and choose the order.
    2. Compute \(\ExpectedCheckingTime\) per attempted record and per admitted record for the current and chosen orders (equation 1), and the fraction of verifier-slot work the change saves.
    3. A colleague proposes dropping \(\text{chk}_{\text{target}}\) instead, because it lowers checking time per admitted record further. Compute that figure and the new yield, and explain why the comparison with part (b) is not a cost comparison at all.

  3. Three denominators for one mixture (Basic). A retained demonstration pool holds 20,000 direct-success records averaging 6,000 stored tokens and 1,000 selected target tokens, and 5,000 recovery records averaging 30,000 stored tokens and 2,500 selected target tokens.

    1. Compute the recovery class’s share of records, stored tokens, and selected targets, and the context per target token for each class.
    2. The team wants recovery to supply half of the selected targets. Holding total selected targets at the current value, compute the presentations each class needs, how many recovery records must repeat, and the change in processed tokens.
    3. Holding processed tokens at the current value instead, compute the total selected targets at the same half-and-half mixture. State which budget this team should fix when training time is the scarce resource, and what the arithmetic cannot decide.

  4. Spending a sampling budget on contrast (Intermediate). A collector has 8,000 attempts for 1,000 repair fixtures in three difficulty bands: 400 fixtures with per-attempt pass rate \(\AttemptPassProbability=0.1\), 400 with \(\AttemptPassProbability=0.5\), and 200 with \(\AttemptPassProbability=0.9\). Attempts are independent, and each fixture receives \(\IndependentAttemptCount=8\).

    1. For each band and in total, compute the expected number of fixtures that yield at least one success and the expected number of mixed groups (equation 3). Compute the attempts spent per mixed group.
    2. A colleague proposes dropping the easy band and spending its 1,600 attempts on the hard band at \(\IndependentAttemptCount=12\). Compute the new expected number of mixed groups and state what the imitation corpus loses.
    3. Decide whether to adopt the proposal for a group-relative objective and for an imitation artifact, and explain why the easy band’s all-success groups are not wasted attempts.

  5. Reviewing after selection (Intermediate). A cohort makes \(\IndependentAttemptCount=10\) independent attempts on each of 200 tasks. Each attempt succeeds with probability 0.2 and costs $0.50 for generation, verification, and storage. Every successful attempt goes to human target review at $6, which passes 75 percent of the successes it sees, independently. The selector emits at most one artifact per task, the first passing success in attempt order.

    1. Compute the expected number of emitted artifacts, the cohort cost, and \(\CostPerEmittedArtifact\) (equation 5).
    2. The team instead reviews a task’s successes in attempt order and stops at its first pass. Show that the set of emitting tasks is unchanged, and compute the expected reviews per task, the new cohort cost, and \(\CostPerEmittedArtifact\).
    3. A second proposal halves \(\IndependentAttemptCount\) to 5 with the stopping review in place. Compute \(\CostPerEmittedArtifact\) and the emitted count, and decide between the two proposals, naming what the cheaper unit price costs the corpus.

  6. The research agent’s collector (Intermediate). The research agent reads untrusted sources, so each collection record keeps the retrieved pages that its claims cite, and the permitted-use review of that retained content is expensive. A collector runs 32 accelerators at 40 accelerator-seconds per attempt, checks attempts on 16 verifier slots, and writes records of at most 1 GB to a store that sustains 500 MB/s (decimal units). The independent predicates take and pass as follows: record adequacy \(\text{chk}_{\text{record}}\), 0.02 s and 98 percent; permitted use \(\text{chk}_{\text{use}}\), 6 s and 60 percent; citation grounding \(\text{chk}_{\text{out}}\), 3 s and 70 percent; target review \(\text{chk}_{\text{target}}\), 2 s and 90 percent. No predicate depends on another’s result. Use the saturated planning model with sufficient end-to-end credits, no additional shared service bottleneck, and each write charged at its record ceiling; the calculated rate is not a measurement of actual collector throughput.

    1. Order the predicates by equation 2 and compute \(\ExpectedCheckingTime\) and the yield \(\AdmissionRouteYield\).
    2. Evaluate equation 4, name the binding stage in this model, and compute its planned admitted records per hour. Compute the write bandwidth that would let generation bind, and the admitted rate then.
    3. The binding stage differs from the repair collector of notebook 15.6. Explain why, and evaluate a proposal to cut the record ceiling to 100 MB by truncating retrieved pages. Propose a design that lowers stored bytes without weakening \(\text{chk}_{\text{record}}\) or \(\text{chk}_{\text{use}}\).

  7. Staging production feedback for the support agent (Advanced). The support agent serves customers who are waiting, so its collector captures production sessions it cannot delay or pause. Sessions finish at \(\ArrivalRate=50\) per second and last 120 s, with capture running throughout. A policy-grounding judge assesses each captured record on \(\WorkerCount=40\) slots in a mean of 0.6 s. Records have a ceiling of 250 KB, and staging holds \(\StagingStoreCapacity=4\) GB and at most 10,000 outstanding records (decimal units). During an incident the judge’s endpoint degrades and every assessment runs to its 10 s timeout. The controller needs 20 s to act, and staging starts nearly empty.

    1. Compute the nominal utilization, the degraded capacity, and the staging growth rate. Compute the time until the byte limit and until the record-count limit is reached, and state which binds.
    2. Size the high watermark on the binding limit, counting records from sessions already capturing and growth during the controller’s delay.
    3. Backpressure on the source is unavailable here, unlike in the repair collector. Compute the capture fraction that holds staging steady during the incident, and critique a proposal to keep capturing only the shortest sessions. State what the corpus manifest must record about the uncaptured sessions.

  8. Designing a leakage screen within a review budget (Advanced). A corpus of 100,000 records is screened with 128-component MinHash signatures before split assignment (section 15.4.3). Assume that 2,000 record pairs are true relatives with Jaccard similarity 0.8 and that every other pair has similarity 0.1. Reviewers can inspect 20,000 candidate pairs per screening pass.

    1. For banding designs of 32 bands of 4 rows, 16 of 8, and 8 of 16, compute the expected false candidates among unrelated pairs and the recall of true relatives. Decide which designs fit the review budget.
    2. Redesign the screen so that it keeps the recall of the 32-by-4 design and fits the budget, using the full signature. Justify the chosen threshold with the estimator’s standard error, and estimate the machine work it adds.
    3. Name a relationship that no choice of bands will catch, and the mechanism that must catch it instead.

What’s Next: From corpus membership to learning targets
Translating an admitted recovery record into a gradient update requires reinforcing the correction rather than the refused proposal it conditions on. Trajectory Fine-Tuning opens with the record this chapter published to repair_demonstrations_v1, whose targets recovery_targets_v1 already fixed. It first asks which parts of the repair behavior belong in weights at all, then turns the curation contract into training examples. Serialization must reconstruct each prefix exactly as the agent received it, loss masking must assign direct supervised loss terms to the selected targets alone, and batching must keep independent examples from reading one another. Preference learning applies a different objective to justified comparisons. Curation decides which material is eligible and why, and the next chapter decides how that material shapes an update and whether the changed policy earns a release under the same runtime controls.
